CE 2 McDOUGALL'S Patent SELF—RAISING FLOUR ANAYS USEDD. The Examiner Saturday, 1 June 17 1905. WOMEN'S CHAT. WOMAN'S REALM. THE BLACK _ _ The Swedish People and Wedding Gifts.—lt is not DRESS-FASHION-HOME. M 0 TOR CAR. the custom among the scandinavian people, to give wedding presents, andithis accounts for what would • otherwise be tbe strange circumstance that Princess Y J. B. HARRIS BURLAND. Margaret has not received any gifts from members of the Swedish Royal fam;ly. An exception to this rule was however, made by the Swedish and, Norwegian residents in this country, who have presented the Princess with a silver-gilt dessert service, out of regard for our national custom orr such occasions. The gift was presented through the Swedish minister, and it was not allowed that the offering should be in any way marred by the political separation which has taken place between the two Scandinavian kingdoms. The only evidence of this event at the Royal wedding, was afforded by. the fact that one or two Norwegians in the suite of Prince Gustar Adolf were replaced by Swedish officials. It is not likely that the action taken by Norway will greatly disturb the peace a mind of the young Prince and Princess, but it is viewed in the- different light /of a national disaster by King Oscar. One result will be to deprive him of a considerable part of his Civil List, as, hitherto the King has received about £73,000 annually from Sweden, and about £27,000 !tom Norway, though it is possible that Sweden will Make up this difference in the royal allowance. Princess Margaret's Wedding Gifts.—The wedding presents received by Princess Margaret were exception- ally numerous an{ beautiful, and made a magnificent display when set forth in one of the drawing-rooms at Clarence House, when the Duke and Duchess of Connaught gave an afternoon party to view them. The Queen, the Prince of Wales, and many members of the Royal Family were early visitors,and Princess Margaret was kept busily engaged in pointing out interesting gifts. Rubies are the favourite gems of the young Princess, and the King and Queen presented her with a beautiful crown and necklace of these stones, surrounded by diamonds. The Duke and Duchess of Connaught pre their daughter a diamond crown, some furs,a silver mirror, and other handsome presents. The Khedive and Empress Eugenie both sent beautiful and costly gifts, and a necklet of rubies and diamonds, which was much admired, was the joint gift or the Prince and Pr' ncess of Wales, the Duke and Duchess of Fife, Princess Victoria, and Prince and Princess Charles of Denmark. Another combination gift of jewels was given by the Duchess of Saxe-Coburg, and her four daughters. There were altogether three large cases of jewels of fascinating interest,and also a host of other gilts which testify to the great general popularity of the young

Princess. A Distinguished Visitor.—The Khedive of Egypt, who was so interesting guest at the Royal wedding will be one of Sir Ernest Cassel's house party for Ascot week. There is little of the Oriental about him, for be was educated in Europe. He is a great friend of Sir Ernest Cassel, who has large interests in Egypt, and the Khedive has also been entertained, whilst In London, by the Earl and Countess of Londatborough, who spent the greater part of last winter at Cairo. Lady Lortdes- borough is a hostess of considerable importance, and her halls and dinner parties are considered among the best in London Society. She was Lady Grace Fane,and is sister to Lord Westmorland. Formerly Londes— borough used to rent Alington House when in London, i but this fine mansion !tow belongs to him,and the family have recently taken up their residence there for the season. The Secularisation of Sunday.—Whilst with us the , secularisation the Sabbath proceeds apace, the; tendency on thelrontinent is to make Sunday more a day of rest, than of pleashre and excitement. It is possible that we, having gone from one extreme to another, may witness a similar reaction from the now generally accepted view' that Sunday, is the most con- venient day of the week for balls, dinners, card parties, and various other entertainments and outdoor amuse- ments. For this state of things, there is no doubt that the fashionable world has had much to do. If any one class is more to blame than another for the disappear- ance of the old-fashioned Sunday, it is the new ariatocmcy of finance and industry, which has per- meated the aristocracy, but however this may be, it is not surprising that when people of wealth and rank make Sunday pleasuring fashionable, other classes who have not all the week to themselves, follow their example. From the gloom of the mid-Victorian Sabbath, we have gone to the other extreme, and it is now common to refer to the week end as dull and wasted if *he Sunday has not brought its usual pleasures and excitements. Concerning Sunshades.—The dressy sunshades are this year more entrancing than ever, with their soft, cloudy chiffon and lace frills, and becoming linings. Cream linsar with rose pink is the leading combination, and undoubtedly this greatly enhances the beauty of the : eyes and complexion. For less ceremonious occasions., plain silk parasols with a border design carried out in ribbon work, or the linen parasol shoring much embroidery and scalloped edges, are likely to enjoy a great vogue—that is, when it becomes safe to lay aside the stout and trusty umbrella, which so far this year has proved almost invariably indispensable when taking our walks abroad. The taste for a startling red, or a bright periwinkle-blue sunshade or cutout cat, is deemed a questionable one, and save at Henley, such noticeable varieties are not likely to be carried by well-dressed women. As for the handles, the best range through pearl—clear and smoked, ivory, gold, copper, and gun- metal, set with every kind of real or imitation gems. Very little in the shape of "freaks" or humorous designs is shown in the leading emporiums, and the sticks are longer than they have been during the past two or three seasons. Veils Fashionable.—The vogue for the real lace veil, which is draped round the hat or toque, and thrown back, so as to form a "curtain," is one of the most charming yet devised, in the millinery world. The-veil may be either in black, white, or cream, or the tint which age— or art—affords, but real lace alone should be utilised for such a pnrpose.—Moreover it is only with a dressy afternoon toilette that this draped veil can be suitably worn. For all other occasions, veils are gathered about the brim, and beneath the chin, and tied neatly behind, the ends being tucked away out of sight. Save on the few hot days we have experienced i this summer, veils have been in great request and there is no doubt that the average woman benefits by this particular fashion. Dust Wraps.—The craze for white has brought about a considerable demand for dainty dust wraps,and those simple varieties being offered just now, in cream alpaca, and tusso e silk, will prove an excellent investment to the girl or woman who has an affection for white apparel, but who is forced to regard it from an economical point of view. Even in town, white and cream is by no means the extravagant wear it was once deemed, but a pretty covering should always be avail- able to slip over such a toilette, when going by train, omnibus, or car, to and from one's destination. Such a wrap is by no means difficult of manufacture at home, but countless bargains in this direction will be offered at the July sales,when doubtless many will be secured , at less than the fabric alone would cost. Frayed Linen.—A laundress who is as successful as I most of her class in shortening the life of linen garments she is entrusted with, says that it is a mistake to trim the frayed edges of cuffs and collars with scissors. The ' better way is to take a lighted taper, and burn off the loose threads. If this is done she says the articles will stand much more of her treatment and still be wearable than is the case when scissors are applied. Laundresses as a role are suposed to take a delight, in the destruc- tion or linen, but this one is evidently more kindly disposed than the msjority of her class, and the hint she gives may be useful to many of U 3 who suffer at their hands. .

.lADGE.

WOMEN AS JEWELLERY WORKERS.

Jewellery working is one of the most satisfactory artistic crafts for a lady to practise, says the Lady. The work will probably be congenial, since gold and silver and precious stones are things that most women lore to handle; and the craft is a light one, involving the use of no heavy or unwieldy tools. The most necessary qualities for a worker are patience, neatness, and great attention to detail, and these no cultured woman ought to find it difficult to give.

CHAPTER XVII. THE COUNTESS OF HEATHERSTONE.

ONE winter's evening, a few weeks after the

events narrated in the last chapter, two women sat in the Countess of Heatherstone's boudoir. The atom was on the first floor and looked over the marshes to the sea. A> door ' opened from it into another apartment, which was used as a dining-room; Another door opened from this into a bedroom, the same room where Jordison had tried • his prentice hand at burglary. , . For two years the whole world, to the Countess of Heatherstone, had consisted of these three roams and the semi-circle of country which she could see from her windows, and the latter was but -a fairyland, untrod, unvisited, and no more real than the scenes portrayed in books'and pictures. She never descended from her apartments to the whirl of society that moved through the palatial reception rooms .beneath her. She had no part. in her husband's life. She received no visitors save those of her own family. Strangers coming to the house for the first time scarcely_ knew of her existence. For all practical purposes she might have been dead. Mistress of the most magnificent establishment in the east of England, she saw none of its glories and held converse with none of the great leaders of men that came to stay under its roof., Her, husban,l, kind and sym- pathetic, came to see ner three times a day. The hours were marked out and observed with strict regularity. He came to the minute, stayed exactly half an hour, and departed. Her step-daughter came in and out at all times. Her presence, was a ray of sunshine, but the sick woman was not sorry to be left 'to her own darkness. The fair and beautiful girl served only to remind her of *hat she herself had been. Her son, the only thing she loved on earth, was dead.

Lady Heatherstone was a comparatively healthy woman, and the doctor promised her long years of life. But two years previously a terrible and loathsome disease had attacked her, and left such frightful ravages on her face that she would not even let her husband look upon it. For two years no living soul had seen beneath the black silk hood that veiled it from the crown ,of her hair to her shoulders. Lady Heatherstone never unveiled, save when she was alone. It was noticeable that there was only one looking-glass in any of the three rooms, and that this was covered with a thick piece of black velvet. In this manner she had resolved to live until death released her. It was a living death, but she had not the strength to face the world again. In years gone by she had been the most beautiful woman in the county. Now the veriest scarecrow among women could thank God that she had not the face of the great Countess of Heatherstone.

On this particular evening Lady Agnes Cliffe had looked in to chat with her stepmother for a few minutes before going to bed. The Countess sat at-, a large rosewood and ormolu table, arranging a pile of russia and morocco gather cases in front of her. In one hand she held a pencil, and opening one case after another, she ticked off the various items on a piece of paper. Lady Agnes sat a couple of yards away, and watched her step-mother listlessly. Her face was very white and tired. All the brightness seemed to have died ont of it. Life had begun to deal hardly with this Young girl, who had hitherto known so little of sorrow. The first blow fell when Lord Overcliffe was killed, the second when Arthur Holme said " Good-bye " to her for ever. The third, a thousand tunes wore terrible', more certain and more irrevoca- ble, when she heard that his yacht had been found dnfting off the Essex coast, and that the dead body of Outen had been picked up near the mouth of the Long Haven Creek. It was quite clear to her that he was dead, though the inquest on Outen gave no clue to the mystery of the death of either men. There was no doubt that the sailor had been drowned, and the Coroner's jury expressed no further opinion on the matter. The " Rover " had 'certainly not foundered, and it seemed inexplicable that her two occupants, both 'experienced sailors, should have come to grief in fair weather. The mystery was in- tensified by the fact that the boat was found drifting with all her sails stowed, and her anchor on board.

telpfece and placed the other in her pocket. Then she took the revolver from the drawer

Lady Agnes had read every word of the evidence, and though it told her nothing, it seemed to leave no room for any hope. Un- doubtedly Arthur Holme was dead, and one day the sea would give up the body to the shore.

The Countess of Heatherstone knew her step - daughter's story, and was not ill-pleased with the termination of so unpromising a love affair. She cared very little for the girl's future happiness, but the traditions of the great Heatherstone family had been ground into her soul, and she had no sympathy with a mesalliance. She was not altogether sorry that a clbud was blotting out some of the sunshine of a fair young life. The clouds were very dark above her own head, and the thought of another woman's happiness was gall to her.

This evening, however, she had no thought for anything but the blue, crimson, and green leather cases in front of her. They were of all shapes and sizes, but each one was stamped Kith the Heatherstone crest and coronet. A rewomore faded than the others, and relics of a more ostentatious period, were impressed with the full armorial bearings of the family—coat of arms, crest, coronet, supporters, and motto. Every one of these cases, and there were one hundred and fifteen in all, contained jewels. Specimens of nearly every gem of the earth were hidden in these little leather shrines. Rings, brooches, bracelets, and tiaras flashed one by one into view as the Countess raised the covers and checked the contents on her piece of paper. She looked at each one lovingly, though the sight of them was not un- tinged with sorrow. As wife of the Earl of Heatherstone she had dazzled many a jealous eye with their splendour, and moved through many a stately room encrusted with their rainbow fire. Now they were for her eyes alone. She idly remembered how one news- paper in the days of her beauty had spoken of iier " Venus, masquerading as the Queen of Sheba."

of the escritoire, and turning out the lights, made her way to her bedroom.

Lady Agnes watched her step-mother in silence. Like most women, she loved jewels, but her thoughts were far away, and she scarcely noticed the flash of emeralds, diamonds, and rubies, as case after case was opened and closed. Once, however, her attention was forced by some remark of her step-mother's about the famous pearl neck- lace. Then for a moment she grasped the whole scene and shuddered. She saw nothing of the lustrous beauty of the pearls, but only the grotesque horror of the masked face glar- ing at the splendid ornament which would probably rest in its case till the owner was lead.

At last the Countess finished her task, and rolding up the pieec of paper, she went to an escritoire, and placed it in one of the drawers. " They're all here, Agnes," she said. "They g 9 to the bank to-morrow. Your father insists upon it." "He is probably right," Lady Agnes re- plied. "After what has happened it would be unsafe to leave them here."

"N- i es, t would certainly be unsafe. They will come again tor them." " Of course, it is better to put them in the bank," said Lady Agnes quietly; "they are determined to have them, and they will take your life if it stands in their way." The Countess\ cipenecl one of the drawers of the escritoire and .pulled out something that glittered. It was a large nickel plated re- volver, no toy, but a serviceable weapon. " I keep this for them," she said ; "it. is loaded, and lies under my pillow at night. I pray for them to come. Ido not want the jewels removed to a place of safety. They will not come it the bait is taken away. Awl Overcliffe has yet to be avenged." A look of fear crossed Lady Agnes' face. She wondered how her step-moth ew would fare in a contest with a desperate man who would be, in all Probability. fully armr,d

Then she turned the two keys in the look that cowed his fierce spirit. He felt that lock, threw one_intts a china vase on the man- he was at a disadvantage, for he could not see - . . .

her face, or read whit was passing in her mind. He had no answer to give to her . . .

questions. But he had to speak-, if only to gain time. "You know well enough what I want," he replied sullenly, after a pause, "and it doesn't matter to you who I am." She continued to gaze at him. Then she laughed. "No," she said slowly. I don't care what your name is. I think I have seen you before. It is enough for me that you are a thief and—a mtirderer. ' She brought the last word out with so ominous an emphasis that Jordison's • blood ranocold. He knew well enough now what he had to expect, and that a lioness robbed of her cubs would be more merciful than the woman who stood before hitd. "A murderer," she repeated in a cold, even voice, 'the imurderer of my son, of my only child. He was all that I had in the world. For two iyears I have endured a living death, and the sight of him was all that kept me from killing myself. Now he is dead, and it is you who have killed him." The cold sweat gathered on Jordison's brow. He could not see the speaker's face, but the eyes were like those of a basilisk, turning him to stone. The chill of death was in her voice ; but under the cold judicial tone of her speech lay some awful volcano of thought, intense, vibrating with passionate hatred, hot as the very fires of hell. He saw that he had fallen into a trap, and that he would be shot like a wild beast without mercy. He glanced round the room, endeavouring to formulate some plan of escape, but only two things were clear to him—the nickel-plated revolver and the pitiless eyes that watched every expression on his face. He knew, how- ever, that he must retain his presence of mind, and, striving to affect an air of indifference, he smiled, and leant carelessly against the wall. "You are mistaken, Lady Heatherstone," he said, pleasantly," I did not kill your son." "Not with your own hands," she muttered, "perhaps not with your own hands; • but you saw him die. One does not make such nice distinctions with a gang of murderers. The one who does the deed, does it for all his fellows. My son's death lies at your door, and I thank God that He has given you into my hands." ; "What do you intend to do? " asked Jordison. He needed no answer to this question, but he still wished to gain time. "I am going to kill you," she replied. " I ought to hand you over to the hangman, but I will spare you the horrors of our penal system, the black cap, the open grave, the whine of the chaplain, the burial service read over a living man. I will be merciful, and shoot you • myself. Perhaps I shall rest when I have avenged my on Each night he cries out to me from his grave." He was silent. His mind harked back to the days of the Inquisition, whex masked figures, like the one before him, sat in judgment and consigned heretics to the torture of fire and steel. Tin very thought of death was agony to him. Life had no pleasure for him, but there was more than one duty to done before he died. By a strange irony (.1 fate the avenger was to become the victim of a vengeance as stern and unsparing as his own. , He moved his right hand from behind his back, and looked. at the ring upon his fore- finger—the fiat opal given him by Marie de la Mothe, once a pledge of love, now the symbol of undying hatred. He set his teeth at the sight of it, and resolved to have a last fling for life. He pressed it to his lips, in no spirit of tenderness, but as a knight of old might have kissed his sword before he went into battle. , It was a kiss that tasted of blood. I The Countess of Heatherstone 'watched the movement, but it inspired her with no pity for her Attim. If this man had someone whc roved him, some wife, some sweetheart who had given him the ring, so much the better. The woman should suffer as she herself had suffered. (To be continued.) '

TRAVELLING Cosrumz.

Dark-blue mohair is the material in which this useful travelling costume is achieved. The simple bolero-coatee of wide stitched - down pleats is adorned with a deep sailor collar, com- posed of a small inner velvet collar and a wide band of fiat silk pleats, held in place by strap- pings of mohair. Similar bands of pleating are placed on the culla of the leg-of-mutton sleeves.

The bell-shaped skirt is very wide at the bottom, and sets well out from the feet. Si s inches above the hem a band of the silk pleating is in- serted, and is bordered top and bottom with strappings of mohair. A pretty hat of blue straw, trimmed with a wreath of creamy roses and foliage, and with a large cach&peigne of blue velvet behind, completes this neat and lady- like gown.

WOMEN AS BOOKING-CLERKS.

Are women booking-clerks desirable!" In the opinion of the chairman of the Railway Clerks' Association they are; but we fancy, says the Bystander, that that opinion will not be generally endorsed. We are not speaking as misogynists when we say that, for our own part, we cannot endorse it. We are speaking merely as individuals who have occasionally to catch trams. It is not that women lack the necessary intelligence to be booking-clerks. So far as intelligence goes, women can do anything. They can be poets, artists, doctors, or typists. A woman Prime Minister is not beyond the bounds of our conception. A woman booking-clerk is another matter. A booking. clerk ought to be as near an automaton as a human being can be. An automaton would, in fact, be a good deal better for the purpose of issuing such , things as tickets than any human being. But if human beings must be employed for the purpose, let them be men. Women cannot be automatic for any length of time. _

===== 0002601_19050617_art0008.txt | included=true | included =====
A LINEN COAT.

Coats of linen are becoming very popular. They are worn over dainty summer dresses, and even those of silk, and look very smart for driving, or to wear when a wrap is necessary to protect the dress. The one shewn in the sketch is composed of coarse white linen. The slight ful- ness is arranged in small tucks at the waist and in the centre of the back. The long basque and the upper part of the coat are made separately,

and attached to a narrow band, which is bidden by a wider belt of green kid. A strap of the linen outlines the neck and the fronts of the coat, and is ornamented with linen-covered buttons, which are worked over with a thick linen thread. Over the shoulders there is a shaped yoke of broderie Anglaise, outlined by narrow straps of linen. The same trimming is also seen on the loose cape sleeves, which are open at the side, and slietly held together wits buttons.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BIi.:DE'S GARTF.R.

It is an old custom at the Prussian Court for the guests at the wedding of the Crown Prince to divide between them the garter of the Princess Royal. Formerly the Princess herself used to take off one of her garters and cut it up for distribution among the guests, but nowadays a special pair is made ornamented with ribbons, on which are embroidered in gold and silk the arms of the bride surmounted by the Royal crown. After the ceremonies are over the Grand Mistress of the _ _ _ _

Court takes one of these garters out of it case-, curs it up, and distributes the pieces to the princi- pal guests. The other garter and the case are put away in the archives of the Royal House of Prussia, where there is a complete collection of all the garters of the Royal and Imperial Princesses. The pair which hgured at the wedding of the Duchess Cecilia were made at Schwerin, and the bride herself designed the coat-of-arms which IS emblazoned on them.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

Idotrrow A LA PORTMAISE. Cut a sufficient number of slices from a cold roast leg of mutton. Trim them into neat fillets. Reserve. Pass the trimmings twice through the mincing machine. Add to them a heaped tablespoonful each of finely. minced parsley and shallot and a large, finely. minced mushroom. Mix. Add a tablespoonful of breadcrurnbs, pepper and salt to taste, and a very little f. rated lemon-rind. Bind with a well-beaten egg. fake a sufficient number of tomatoes, allow- ing one for each person. Cut a small piece from the top of each, and scoop out the inside. (Reserve the latter on a plate.) Fill the tomatoes with the farce, and place a bit of butter on top of each. Cook in a hot oven for twenty minutes. Reserve. Place a bit of butter about the size of a walnut in a clean stewpan. As soon as it oils, add the mutton, together with as much finely-minced garlic . as will go on the point of a small knife. Fry for five minutes. Add the contents of a bottle of tomato ratsuo. the tomato pul and neoner and M salt to taste. ate very not. [Han up the meat on a hot dish. Pour the sauce over. Garnish with the stuffed tomatoes, and serve at once. Fried or baked potatoes should be banded separately.

CHAPTER XVIIL FACE TO FACE.

THE Countess of Heatherstone had firmly resolved not to sleep that night. It was the last night that the Heatherstone jewels would be in the house. Lord Heatherstone, in spite of her own wishes, had blazoned this infor- mation over the whole county. He had no wish for his house to be any longer the happy hunting ground of the criminal classes. It was certain that if the thieves intended to make another attempt, they would make it that night. Lady Heatherstone wished to be awake to receive them.

But are, who follows her own fixed laws, and takes small count of human wishes, had ordained otherwise, and Lady Heatherstone was sound asleep in less than half an hour. The mask was laid aside, and her right hand grasped the revolver under her pillow. The merciful darkness shrouded her, as she lay there with bare face, and her fingers on the weapon. About two o'clock she woke from her sleep with a start. A wild dream of vain pursuit after her: son through long tracts of desolate and mountainous country had terminated in a catastrophe. Lord Overclitle had flung him- self from the edge of a precipice; she had grasped at him as he disappeared, slipped, and sank swiftly downwards through the Ektrlcness. Her very soul and life seemed to be drawn from her as she dropped through the air like a stone. The bottom rose to meet her—a lake of fire dotted with jagged rocks. But she never reached the bottom. She awoke, gasping for breath, bathed in a cold perspiration, trembling in every limb. She switched on the electric light by the edge of her bed, and peered round the room, half expecting to see Lord Overcliffe swim- ming away from her through thai lake of fire. But she only saw the white and gold decora- tions of her bedroom, and, sitting up, she began to grasp the realities of life, and to understand that she had been sleeping at the post of duty. She sat up in her bed and listened. At first she heard nothing but the ticking of the clock on the mantelpiece. Then she heard a sound that sent the blood more quickly through her veins—a low grating sound, as though someone were cutting laboriously through a plate of glass or steel. She crept quietly out of bed, flung a robe of black velvet lined with sable,about her, and put the hood over her face. Then she slipped the revolver into one of the pockets of her dressing-gown, switched off the electric light and went to the door that opened into the dining-room. Here she listened attentively, and the noise seemed clearer and more definite. She looked through the keyhole, but saw no trace of light. Then she opened the door softly add crept into the dining-room. The noise grew still more distinct. She crossed the room on tip-toe, and looked through the keyhole of the door that led into her boudoir. The small dimensions of the aperture limited her range of vision, and she could see nothing save that there was a light in the room. The noise had ceased. She wondered if the door was locked. She turned the handle very % softly and slowly and pulled it towards her. The door opened half an inch, and she let the handle turn quietly back into Its original place. She smiled at the artless- ness ,of the thief within. An accomplished butglar would surely have locked all the doors before he commenced his work. She looked through the narrow slit and saw a man kneeling before the safe. A small' lantern threw a circle of light a foot in dia- meter on the steel door. He was holding something in his hand close to the lock. It IJoket.l like a small penholder, with two thin tubes running from it into the darkness behind him. Then she saw him 'light a match and (apply it to the ooint of the pen holder. A thin

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Increase In County Expenditure.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday, when there were present :—Messrs. A. R. Norman (presiding), A. Greenway, J. Bolshaw, H. Eyes, G. Egerton-Warburton, J. Stokes, J. Whitlow, T. Baxter, H. M. Davies, C. E. Linaker, J. H. Davies. J. Smith, T. Wright, T. Guest, S. Woodward, W. Harrison, G. Pilling, S. Horton J. L. Jones E. Bretherton, J. Spruce, P. Collins, J. Hallows, J. Whitley, B. Billington, C. E. Linaker,jun.,T.Antwis, Admiral Jones-Parry, and Messrs. G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Cunnah (master), W. de Prez, Cook and Pearson (releiving officers).

TIM 'UNEMPLOYED QIIRSTION. The following resolution was read from the Fulham Board of Guardians: That it is most inexpedient for national welfare, contrary to the best interest Of the state and especially detrimental to the poor themselves by tending to reduce their self reliance and independ- ence that a system of relief supported by municipal rates should be established in addition to that outside the existing poor law.

On the m-otion of Mr. Woodward it was decided to support the resolution of the Fulham Board.

UNDERFED SCHOOLCHILDREN. - The following letter was read from the Keighley Union: That this Board petition the Local Govern- ment Board to alter or revoke the relief (school children) Order 1905, with a view to arrange that some other constituted body and not the Board of Guardians should be the medium through which out-relief should ' be given to underfed school children, and thttt the cost of such relief should be a charge on the National Exchequer. The giounds for the passing of the resolu- tion were as follows : (I) The feeding of school children under the direction of Guardians would lead } to pauperise them and induce a spirit on dependence , of entertaining life and at an age when permanent habits are formed. (2) The fathers of the children would be disfranchised against their will. At a time when they may be struggling Against temporary adversity. Local rates are already excessive and the burden is more expecially felt in poor districts. The feeding of i children s a national object and the cost should be derived out of a national fund to which the public contribute more according to their ability. Mr. Woodward said he failed to see bow the proposal could be carried out without increasing the expendi- ture as it would mean a multiplication in the legislative 'machinery.

It was decided to allow the letter to lie upon the table.

tiICREASE IN COUNTY EXPENDITURE. Mr. Woodward pointed out that there had been an increase in the County Council. For the past half year the calls had been £7,596 as against £7,030 for the previous half year. The principal increase was in re- gard to general elementary education. The total amount of the call for the purpose during the past half year had been £3731 as against £2,798 for the previous-half year. He did not know whether there was any connec. tion between education and police but while the educa- tion expenditure had increased the police district wenditure had gone up f0r4859 to £BB2. For general police purposes the expenditure had gone up from £233 to £349.

Mr. Baxter : We were told some years ago that if we educated the people we would do away with police rates.

The Chairman said he did not think there was anything abnormal in the increase in the expenditure for pnlice purpohes. LADIES VISITING COMMITTEE. Mesdames T. Lee Jones, White, Mc Iver and Trampleasure, were added to the ladies visiting com- mittee. •

Mr. Spruce reported that the number of inmates lit the house was 126 as compared with 132 for the corres- ponding period of last year.

NURSERIES, NOT SCHOOLS. Speaking at Knutsford, Dr. Garstang, the medica officer, said it bad long been his opinion and the opinion of other medical officers that it was a pure farce to send childrsn under five years of age to school. Their Intellects and trains were not sufficiently developed,and many infant departments he had visited were nothing more than nurseries.

CHESHIRE WEDDING TRAGEDY. The West Cheshire Coroner on Wednesday morning held an inquest at Tarporley on George Clarke Gregory, wheelwright, aged 96, who died under distressing cir- cumstances. Two villagers were married on Monday, and deceased, the oride's brother, and others, fired salntes on the swage, or blacksmith's punch block. A fuse broke, and an sxplosion occurred, practically blow- ing away half of Gregory's face.—The Coruner hoped the dangerous practice would be discontinued. The verdict was "Accidental death."

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

WIDNES GAS PROFITS.

The Widnes gasworks, it was reported to the T6wn Council on Tuesday, produced last year a profit of £6,135, though the gas is the cheapest in the world— Is. :6d. per thousand feet to private consumers and Is. 2d. tolmanufacturers. Production and distribution cost Ild, per thousand feet, and last year the residuals more than paid for the coal used.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
DOCK WAREHOUSE DESTROYED.

At Runcorn, on Monday evening, a large warehouse at Runcorn Docks belonging to the Manchester Ship Canal Company was completely destroyed by fire, only the pillars being left standing. Crates of pottery were stored in the upper storey and china clay on the ground floor, and the damage is set down at several thousand pounds. Two flats belonging to the Anderton Company, Stoke, were also set on fire and badly damaged. There is no explanation of how the tire originated.

VICTORY OVER DISEASE.

Severe Slomaeh Troubles Cured by Mother Selgel's Syrup—Mrs. Gould Tells of it 12 Years Later.

Mrs. Sarah Ann Gould, living at 22. Borron Street Portwood, Stockport, Cheshire, is a woman in middle life, hale and hearty-looking now, though eleven years ago she was so ill from indigestion and general stomach troubles, that she and bet friends alike feared that she could not live. But. she did live, and she confidently gives all the credit for her cure and recovery to Mother Seigel's Symp. People who are afflicted as she was, whether more or less severely, may profit by reading, Mrs. Gould's own description of her symptoms, as sh*, told them in a letter which she sent on February 15th 1895. to the proprietors of Mother Seigel's remedies. She said her trouble began early in 1888, seventeen years ago, with a weak tired feeling, inability to enjoy' her food, a belching up of wind from th stomach, and a raising of sour bile. She took doctor's medicines,but only grew worse, and began to suffer intense pains in the head and stomach, and lost both flesh and strength, At lastshe had to keep to h.r bed, and was lifted in and out. In November, 1891, she wee advised to try Mother Seigel's Syrup, and after taking it only a short time she began to improve. Her appetite .returned, she could enjoy and digest her food, and she gained her lost strength and flesh. " Presently " sbe wrote, "I was another and a new woman."

Writing again, on December 23rd last, she says: "If you remember, mine was a most severe case. I could not eat, and was so weak I could hardly stand. In fact, I used to faint away from sheer weakness' had bad palpitation of the heart, and suffered much from cold, clammy sweats, which left me thoroughly exhausted; while the heachaches I endured were almost maddening. The doctor saidl could not recover, yet Mother Seigel 's, Syrup completely cured me, and I have baen well ever since.

ST. HELENS

WRITMONDAY PROCESSION.

The Whitsuntide demonstration of the Roman Catholics of St. Helens was favoured with glorious weather. The children gathered in the Victoria Square in front of the Town Hall, and thence in procession made a tour of the town, with a profusion of flags and banner fluttering in the breeze. The girls were, for the most part, in white with sashes and favours of various colours, and roand the principal banners there were clustered groups of children daintily attired,in many cases adorned with flowers. There were some fifteen bands of music, and as the scholars, members or the guild, and so forth, to the number of about 12,000, passed the Town Hell they saluted the Mayor and Mayoress (Mr. and Mrs. H. Martin), who remained on the steps during the hour which the procession took to file by. Thousands of people lined the raute. The churches represented were St. Anne's Sutton; St. Joseph's, Peasley Cross; St. Austin's, 'll;atto Heath; the Sacred Heart, Lowe House; St. Thomas of Canterbury, Blackbrook ; St. Vincent's, Derbyshire Hill; and the Holy the afternoon schools festivals were held. HIS LIFE FOR AN ANIMAL.

On Lake Bret, near Lausanne, a young man was drowned whilst attempting to save a roebuck, which the dogs had hunted into the water. In the struggle to draw the refractory animal to the row-boat the man fell overboard and disappeared, while the roe reached the shore and vanished in the wood.

THE FLABBERGASTER. (Continued from laet week.)

Why is a mouse when it spins? Everybody was puzzled—the King, the Queen, the court- iers, and even the smallest servant. The Lord Chamberlain said it was all Tommy nonsense, for who ever heard of a mouse spinning. The King then recollected that he had never even seen a mouse, therefore could form no idea as to whether it could spin or not, and as he did not go in for guessing competitions, he thought it best to be on the safe side, and see for himself whether a mouse could spin. He commanded one to be brought him. Then followed a Royal mouse-hunt. In a few minutes all were en their knees„ except- ing.the ladies, and they got on the chairs and other things, perhaps to get a better view of the game. At last a mouse was caught whilst in the act of nibbling bread and honey (which the Queen had been eating in the [parlour), and placed before the King, who, after looking at it for some time, said that he couldn't find a place where if could spin upon, unless it was the tail, and even that kept bending. Then the Queen said that a mouse had never been known to spin, and never would. "Rut," said the Court Jester, "your Majesty must remember' that the question is WREN it spins." "Yes, but," continued the. Queen—." "There is no ' but,' " chimed in the King, "and no sense either." "Let's give up guessing," suggested someone. "No," said the King, "let's go on ' with it." So everyone began putting on their thinking caps and remained as quiet as the mouse they had oaught. (To be continued.)

A VERY GOOD GIRL. Our merry little daughter Wa3 climbing out of bed— " Don't you think that I'm a good girl?" Our little daughter said; "For all day long this lovely day, And all day long to-morrow, I baven't done a single thing, To give my mother sorrow!"

The Examiner, Saturday, June 17, 1905. A;

NORTHERN UNION.

ANNUAL MEETING IN MANCHESTER. Abolition of the Working Clauses.

There was a crowded attendance of gentlemen con- cerned in professional Rugby football at the Grosvenor Hotel, Manchester, on Wednesday evening (says "Yorkshire Post ") the occasion being the holding of the annual general meeting of the Northern Rugby Union. Mr. F. Lister' of Bradford, officiated as chair- man. The majority of the clubsin the Union were repre- sented.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
IHE FINANCIAL POSITION.

The report on last season's working was presented by Mr. J. Platt, hon. secretary. A year ago the balance in the bank and at hand stood at £1,931 12s. Bd. On June 1 of the present year it stood at £1,770 3s. 2d., thus show- ing a reduction on the year's working of £l6l 9s. 6d. During the season grants amounting to -£3lB wore made in respect of junior and schools' football, and a sum of £256 is. was disbursed in charitable donations. The Yorkshire county team were to be congratulated upon winning the County Championship. They thoroughly deserved the honour, as they undoubtedly proved them- selves to be the best team of the year. THE WARRINGTON CLUB had for years fought hard forpossession of the Northern Union Cup, and after being beaten in the final of 1904, they last season went one better, and had now the pleasure and honour of being the holders of the trophy. Their victory was a popular one, and was wonsby sheer merit.

The expenses side of the balance-sheet showed one or two interesting features. The semi-final ties of the cup tournament cost the Union £722 14s. 10d., and the final tie £499 10s. 6d., these figures meeting match expenses and proportions of gate receipts claimed by the con- tending clubs. In addition to the grants to junior and schools' football already referred to, a sum of £230 78. 3d. was granted to County Unions. On the income side were three items which collectively amounted to £2,267 9s. Gd., the product of the semi-final and final rounds of the cup-ties. Subscriptions amounted to £72 95., while £382 14s. Sd. was paid to the Union in fines.

On the motion of Mr. Lister, the report and balance- sheet were unanimously adopted. "FLIRTING" WITH THE ASSOCIATION GAME. The next suggested alteration in rules came at the instance of the Northern Union Committee. Last season it was apparent that the Association code was making serious inroads to the professional handling game, and not a little heartburning was caused by the action of the Hull Club in allowing the Hull City Association Club to play on the Boulevard Ground. As a consequence, the Northern Union Committee pro- posed the following resolution :—" That no club shall be represented upon the Union Committee whose interest in football is not solely devoted to the Northern Union game, subject to this proposition not interfering with any existing agreement made prior t) January 1, 1905." To this the Salford Club proposed an amendment, which was seconded by Swinton, to the effect that January 1, should be substituted by September 1. Mr. King, the Salford representative, said they had made certain arrangements before the proposition of the Union was sprung upon them, and this, in their opinion, was extremely unfair. The question was then asked whether the proposed change in the law would apply in the case of a club allowing their ground for an occasional Association match, to which Mr. J. H. Smith (Widnes) replied that if a club did so it would forfeit its seat on the Union Board. . .

Mr. Gresty said he would like to know what position the Union Board would take up in the case of clubs who were not represented. "In my opinion," he added, "the proposition of the Board does not go far enough. (Hear, hear.) Ido not understand how any club can be wedded to the Rugby game, and flirt with the Associa- tion code. They must steer clear of the rival code altogether, or much harm will be done. The proper way to deal with the matter is for the Union to put its foot down firmly, and prevent any joining of forces under Gne roof."

• Mr. Smith said the board were quite in agreement with Mr. Gresty, and the sole reason for not having proposed a sterner resolution was out of consideration for the junior clubs, who found great difficulty in obtaining grounds without, in many instances, assistance from Association dubs.

Mr. J. Platt (Oldham) said the board felt that the question raised by Mr. Gresty ought to be settled by the League Committee. • Mr. Gresty : In my opinion it is the big clubs that require protection. The big clubs have everything at stake, and for that reason require more protection than the weaker clubs, having had to bear the brunt of the Association competition. I hope the League Committee will speedily have the matter under their consideration. (Bear, hear). Mr. Lister now said that the Board had decided to recommend the League Committee to take stronger action

ThE proposition was carried by a large majority. The Union, to safeguard themselves, proposed the following resolution :—"That the committee are the sole interpreters of these rules, and no matter what is dealt with 13, the committee shall be appealed against either at law or otherwise." _ .

- - Mr. Ayre (Hull): Do you think such a rule would hold good in a court of law? Mr. Lister: from the advice we have had I think so.

ANNOYING WORKS CLAUSES ABOLISHED. During last season the employment clauses were a continual source of annoyance both to players and committees. As a consequence a resolution that the works clauses should be abolished was brought forward by the Hull F.C., seconded by the Broughton Rangers F.C. The resolution read as follows—" That the whole of Clauses C, D, and E of the Professional rules be abolished." As was the case last year, the arguments used were those concerning the difficulties experienced in keeping players employed and illustrations were afforded where clubs had suffered great hardships. The clauses were a source of great annoyance, and their abolition would save secretaries a great deal of hard work. Mr. J. Lewthwaite (Elunslet) was against the proposed abolition of the rules. He considered it was dinicult enough for clubs to keep afloat under the present coU- ditions, and if wages had to be paid in the summer months, as was probable, many of them would quickly go to the wall. Mr. Howarth (Millom) said the change would make very little difference to the clubs in his district. They could not pay their players more than a few shillings I per match, but by the abolition of those clauses the I Union would get rid of many difficulties, For this reason he offered the resolution his support. Mr. J. W. Wood (Leeds) offered a dissenting voice to the resolution. He contended that many clubs could not afford to pay men during the summer. " The change," he concluded, "will be the death blow to the game." (Hear, hear.) Mr. Gresty said the interpretation of the clauses had done much to retard the progress of the Union. They had fettered themselves needlessly. The resolution was then put to the vote, and 31 mem- bers voted for it, and 25 against, a majority for the pro- positionof six. A two-thirds majority not being neces- sary in this case, the resolution was carried, and the working clauses are now abolished. SUGGESTED REVIVAL OF THE PUNT OUT.

The Bionghton Rangers Club, through their rdpresen- tative, Mr. Kirkham, moved the following alteration in Rule 12, which concerns touch play The ball is in touch when it, or a player carrying it, touch or cross the touch-line ; in the case of a player carrying the ball bbing pushed or accidentally stepping in touch it shall be taken into play ten yards from and at right angles to the touch-line and there put down and scrummaged; in any other case the ball shall belong to the side opposite to that last playing it in the field of play. One of the side to whom the ball belongs shall bring it into play by punting it from touch at or behind the spot where it crossed the touch-line. The ball may be kicked in any direction, but must not be kicked over the goal-line of the side taking the kick. The kicker's side must be behind the ball when the kick is taken. The opposite side may come up to and charge from anywhere on or behind a line drawn through the spot where the ball crossed the touch-line, and parallel to the goal-line. On any infringement of this law the ball shall belong to the opposite side. As an F. amendment it was proposed by Warrington C. . .

The ball is in touch when it, or a player carrying it touches or crosses the touch-line; in the case of a player carrying the ball being pushed or accidentally stepping in touch, it shall then be taken into play ten yards from and at right angles to the touch-line, and there pet down and scrummaged. In any other case the ball shall belong to the side opposite to that last playing it in the field of play. One of the side to whom the ball belongs shall bring it into play by punting it from touch at or behind the spot where it crossed the touch line. The ball may be kicked in any direction, but must not be kicked over the goal-line of the side taking the kick. The kicker's side must be behind the ball when the kick is taken. The opposite side may may come up to and charge from anywhere on or behind a line drawn through the spot where the ball crossed the touch line, and parallel to the goal-line. On any infringement of thisiaw the ball shall belong to the opposite side. F As an Amendment it was proposed by Warrington .C. : The ball is in touch when it, or a player carrying it, touches or crosses the touch-line; in the case of a player carrying the ball being pushed or accidentally stepping into touch, it shall then be taken into play ten yards from and at right angles to the touch-line, and there put down and scrummaged. In any other case the ball shall be taken back to where last played and there put down and scrummaged. Another amendment waf proposed by Runcorn P.C., seconded by Huddersfield and Wigan P.C.'s. In bringing forward the proposition, Mr. Kirkham and that his club were satisfied that it would be better for the game if the old punt out rule was again brought its force. They were quite satisfied that the present

rule was killing the game. People were not following Northern Union football es they had done in the past, but going over to the Association game. There was far too much scrummaging under the existing rule. Instead of 80 minutes' football he (Mr. Kirkham) contendd that not 50 minutes real open play was seen in the the League games. Mr. King (Salford) also argued that the resuscitation of the punt out rule would make the game more attractive. Under the existing rule it would be im- possible to get back the old followers of the game. It had been said that more rough play was seen when the punt out rule was in force than was now the case. If they would look through their minute books they would find that very few clubs got into trouble over the kick oat. In moving the Warrington amendment, Mr. Ashton said that the rule would keep the ball oftener in the field of play than was at present the Case. Th 3 Broughton proposition was carried when put to the vote. On the proposition being put against the standing rule that, too, was lost, for though 35 voted in favour of it, and 24 against, the necessary two-thirds majority had not been obtained. The existing rules thus stands.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMBERS AND CUP TIES.

- -- - The Broughton Rangers Club, seconded by the Salford Club. proposed the following amendment to Rule 7 of the Challenge Cup tournament : The charge for admission to qualifying and inter- mediate rounds shall be at the discretion of the home club, but for the Cup-ties proper the minimum charge for admission to matches shall be sixpence, except for boys, when the charge shall be twopence. All members and ticket-holders must pay the admission charges to ground and stands for the semi-Anal and final, but for the other rounds only bona-fide members who have the right to vote at their club's general meeting, or subscribers of a not less sum than ten shillings per annum, shall have the right to free admission. Mem- bers of works recreation clubs, who have a deduction made from their wages in support of the various recrea- tions and sports connected with such works, shall not be considered bons-fide members within the meaning of this rule. This was carried by 39 votes to 16. The auditors, Mr. Leonard and Mr. Tyrer, were re-elected for the ensuing year, and the meeting rose after lasting two and a half hours. MR. H. ASHTON'S VIEWS.

The abolition of the working clauses marks a very important gignge in the history of Northern Union football, foMit is the working clauses which hitherto have chiefly distinguished the Northen Union system from any other. Interviewed by an " Examiner " reporter, Mr. H. Ashton, the secretary of the Warring- ton Club, said the matter was so many-sided that it would be difficult to prophesy as to the effect of the alteration. As far as the Warrington chili was concerned he was convinced that it would make little or no differ- ence. An important point to be noted was that very few clubs were in a position to pay high wages to players' and it would therefore be in the men's own interests to the work as the club had to "cut their coat according to their cloth." Of course, said Mr. Ashton, the abolition of the clauses might involve considerable change with one or two clubs who were determined to keep a good team at any cost and were prepared to make sacrifices. Mr. Ashton remarked that a strict watch would be kept on players who did not work, and men who neglected to keep themselves in condition would quickly lose their places on the teams. On the whole he considered that players would be well-advised in continuing to work, and in War- rington he believed the majority would follow that course.

ZAM-BUK, THE PAIN-KILLER. A Dyer's Maddening Affliction

'Completely Banished

Thousands of workers perform their daily task tortured by acute bodily • pains. The woman leaning over the wash-tub, the daughter at the shop or factory, the mechanic at his bench ; in fact all who have to stand and bend for long intervals know the meaning of the word "aches." The experience of Mr. Chas. Henry Long, of 19, Paul-street, Manningham, indicates the real value of Zam-Buk as a pain-killer. To a representa- tive of the " Bradford Daily Telegraph," he said :—" 1 was always blessed with a robust constitution in my youth, but the dye-house is a very hard place to work in. I have to bend a great deal over the vats, and some time ago I commenced to suffer from excruciating pains in the back. This had a very depressing effect on me, and I dreaded almost having to go to my work. The pains started first at the bottom of the back, and then gradually went to the shoulder blades. Subsequently I had severe attacks of headache These pains almost drove me mad, and I tried all kinds of so-called remedies, but I could get little relief. Both backache and headache continued for many months, and I was at times unable to go to my work. I then saw an account in the papers about the remarkable soothing qualities of Zam-Bnk, and gave it a trial. I had my back and shoulders rubbed well with it every night. At first there was little improve- ment, but I continued until I found the pains were dis- appearing. I persevered, and at last Zam-Buk killed them altogether. Now lam as well as ever, and it is a I pleasure to work. I attribute my cure solely to Zam- Buk." Zam-Buk is superior to the old-style embrocations I and liniments. Rubbing Zam-Buk in rubs pain, stiffness, I or weakness out and it should always be used for sore- I ness in joints or muscle, stiffness, sprains, strains, back pains, rheumatism cramp, swelling due to weakness or inflammation, bad knees and ankles, aching feet, cuts, bruises, burns, scalds, poisoned or festering sores, eczema, psoriasis, ulcers, bad legs, diseased ankles, sore backs, itch, dandruff, survy, scalp soreq, erysipelas, piles, rashes, abscesses' boils, pimples, ringworm, &c. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Memorial Service.

A special service was held in the Risley Presbyterian Church on Sunday in memory of the late Rev. W. Dunn of Risley, at which there was a large attendance The Rev. J. L. Milne, of Warrington,ofaciated,and preached from the words " Fallen asleep." He made a touching reference to the labours of the late minister. Being present at the Presbytery, the preacher said Mr. Dunn =dea most vigorous speech on behalf of the Risley Church to prevent its being made a preaching station, and be mightalso say the decased had given his life for his old church. Special hymns were sung for the occa- sion, and the Dead March was render ed by the organist

Proposed Renovation'of Wycliffe Church. This important subject is to be considered at the next church meeting in connection with Wycliffe Con- gregational Church. In the current number of the "Church Record" Mr. Zibs Armitage writes that it is estimated that the work which the deacons are agreed oughtto be undertaken without delay, will require a fund of £750, including the debit balance of £2OO on the last building fund. This " says Mr. Armitage, is a larger amount than was expended in 1890, and will re- quirt larger contributions on the part of an increases number of friends. This is enough to act as a stimulant but nothing to despair about. The motion of which notice has been given refers only to the raising of a subscription, and it is hoped the Church will give its cordial assent and appoint a strong committee for the purpose. so that in the spring or early summer of next year, such progress may have been made in the collec- tion of funds beforehand, t that the Church will then see fit to order the work to be carried out. The Minister and Deacons, by way of giving a lead, have made conditional promises amounting in the aggregate to £175 to the proposed decoration fund." Baptist Church, Warrington. Special services in celebration of the Baptist Sunday School anniversary were held on Sunday. In the morning the Rev. H. Dunnico preached in the church. There was a good congregation. En the afternoon a musical service was held in the Parr Hall, when there was a good congregation. Mr. William Roberts occupied the chair. He said it gave him great pleasure to publicly associate himnelf with the magnificent work Mr. Mimic° was doing. He had watched his work very closely and had attended the services again and again, and he could bear personal estimony to the enormous good which was being done. He did not say that he always agreed with him. He had net, but he admired him none the less for saying what he thought and calling a spade a spade. Mr. Roberts said he owed much to the teaching of the Sunday school. He was not at the present time closely associated with Sunday school work, but h was trying in various ways to make the town be loved a little behtter than he had found it. (Applause). The address was given by Kr- T. Jones, of Bolton, who gave an object lesson onche.racter-building. During the afternoon the Legh-street Primitive Metho- dist choir, under the leadership of Mr. Spruce, rendered two anthems "Hear my prayer "and "The heavens are telling." Miss Bridge and Mr. Corrin were the soloists. Both pieces were rendered with mugh feeling, the render- ing of "Rear my prayer" being well nigh perfect. In the evening the Parr Hall was well filled, the Pastor again preaching. The choir and children, under the leadership of Mr. Trow, gave several anthems and choruses. Master B. Clatworthy presided at the organ. The collections amounted to £4o—a decrease upon those of the previous year.

A HEART-BROKEN MOTHER.—The loss of three sons in the South African war, followed by the threatened death from Consumption of a beloved daughter, was the experience of a Newcastle woman. Fell reference to this case,with the happy sequel as regards saving the daughter's life, will be found in the article headed " CONSUMPTION'S DEADLY HOLD."

41 DAYS ACROSS ATLANTIC. TURBINE LINER VIRGINIAN BREAKS ALL RECORDS.

The Allan Steamship Company on Wednesday evening received a cablegram announcing that their liner Virginian has broken all Atlantic records, having passed Cape Race in four days six hours after leaving Moville. This means that the passengers would be only four days out of land. Since Messrs. Allen introduced turbines on their Liverpool-Canada route they have cut off a complete day in the time occupied in the Atlantic passage.

COMMERCIAL Fatrxn,Es.—According to " Kenip's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of Commercial Failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, June 9th, was 173, viz. : —New bankruptcy proceedings published in the London , Gazette, 96; deeds of arrangement registered, 77. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were: Bankruptcies, 101; deeds of arrangement, 86—total 187; being an decrease of 14. The total number of commercial failures recorded during the 23 weeks of the present year is 4,127 ; the total number recorded in the corresponding 23 weeks of last year was 4,016 shocking an increase of 111.

THE EXAMINER.

JUNE 17th 1905.

Lieut. G ene ral Sir THE ARMY STORES WILLrAm BUTLER has SCANDAL, again rendered enor

moos service to the country by his exposure of the terrible scandals in connection with the War Stores Department in South Africa. Will it be possible, we wonder, for any Tory speaker or writer, after the grave irregu- larities and swindles revealed in Sir WILLIAM BUTLER'S report, to plead any longer that thisl Tory Government has been wise and strong in foreign affairs ! The nation is already familiar with dead meat scandals and remount scandals, but the latest revelations are the most deplorable and discreditable of all. It appears that at the close of the war the military authorities held enormous accumulations of food supplies and forage in South Africa—sufficient to feed more than 300,000 men and '200,000 animals for four months. Lord KITCHENER declared that we should be able to sell locally a great quantity of this material at good prices, and now we learn from the blue book that a gang of =tractors used their influence in official quarters in order to obtain these stores at extremely low prices, and worse still, they immediately resold them to the military authorities at an inflated figure. A profit of £2,000 is said to have been made in one day on a deal in oats, while the sale of chaff in a single instance realised £6l3 moneys which left the imperial treasury for the pockets of unscrupulous speculators. Than them) worthy people were allowed also to reject a quantity of the stores on the ground of "inferior quality,"and to claim and obtain a rebate of £21,232 the payment of which huge sum seems to have been authorised solely by the signature of a subaltern The blue-book further informs us that the cheque for this £21,232 was issued by the Army Pay Department apparently without any questions being asked or any counter signature being required. A pretty story truly ! It is so amazing that one wonders what the Tory press and party would have said if such things had happened under a Liberal administration. Will the Tory Govern- ment resign or will they still cling to office and con- tend that their occupancy of the ministerial benches is necessary for safeguarding the honour of the country abroad and the protection of imperial interests ? We remember that the last Liberal Government was: ejected from office because the Tory party pretended to have discovered a shortage in cordite at home. They considered this a very scandalous breach of confidence and were horrified to think that the national safety might be jeopar- dised by this want of war material, which they perfectly well knew could be rectified at a few hours notice. What have these same conscientious gentlemen, who protested so loudly then, got to say now in mitigation of the scandalous revelations , made since the Tory Government plunged us into Ithe miserable South African business ?

One of the most telling indictments in Sir WILLIAM BUTLER 9 report is as follows : "Are the taxpayers of this country to continue to be the sport of the many ques- tionable contractors who are as ready to follow their several avocations in the wake of a war as they are also willing to be its pioneers?"

Here we find it roundly asserted that these "questionable contractors " were the pioneers of the war. For them we have poured out the life's blood of over 20,000 of our brave song, we have devastated the country of South Africa, destroyed two republics, and handed over the enormous wealth of the diamond and gold fields to alien millionaires who succeeded in getting from this Government of ours permission to introduce the Chinese labourer into the positions which we were given to understand would be open to Britishers at the termination of the war.

Mr. ARNOLD Fosna,Secretary for War, appears to be extremely anxious that the British public I should not too severely censure the " persons named. . . . in the report." He says that "as far as charges against individuals are concerned the matter dealt with in the Report must be regarded as sub judi co." The Report "cannot be regarded as a judicial finding." This is rlear on its surface. The evidence was incomplete, it was not taken on oath, and some of the persons whose conduct is called in question have not been heard. Further search is necessary, we are told, "to reach that hitherto impenetrable background where some deeper calculator has his abode." We can only hope that some sleuth-hounds will yet be able to track to their lair those "chief offenders against the nation to which by birth or nationalisation they belong." The danger is lest some of these men should be allowed to escape altogether from the penalties which their conduct merits, and the chief difficulty lies in the question whether the final investiga- tion should be entrusted to representatives of the present Government. In any event the whole sordid business must eventually, and before long, be salamittel to the national verdict, which, we hope, will be given in such a manner as to show this and all future administrations that they cannot thus trifle with the nation's blood and resources with impunity.

The National Service League is making con- siderable headway locally, judging by the second annual report of the Warrington branch which has 3ust been issued. Thanks to the energy of Captain Crosfield, and the excellent work of the Ladies' Committee, the membership of the branch has nearly doubled during the year and now stands r at 393, while the finances are in a sound condi- tion notwithstanding a good deal of propaganda work. Obviously from the repurt, the National Service Leaguers have not yet forgiven Mr. Balfour for publicly pooh-poohing the idea of a foreign military invasion. "Slowly but surely," says the report, "will the educative influence of the League

make itself felt, despite the misleading arguments of our statesmen." It has all along been one of the pet methods of the League to work upon our fears if we have any, and to create them if we have not. The bogey of invasion has been so often held up before us that it has become like the cry of " wolf" in the fable. We hope, however, that the sequel will not be so sad. Our readers are well aware tha; it is in: regard to the question of compulsory service where we, in the main, fall foul of the League. With its aims of inculcating a spirit of discipline, improving the physique of the nation, and even of imparting military training, we are in full sympathy, so long as the training is voluntary. But unfortunately the League chooses to consider the voluntary system played out, whereas our view is that it has never yet had a fair chance. If the League would devote its energies to obtaining a thorough reformation of the basis and management of our auxiliary and Volunteer forces it would be performing a com- mendable task with much greater likelihood of a widespread public support. Compulsory training " goes against the grain " with the free-born Britisher, though he is never behind-hand when there is•any fighting to be done. While dissenting from the methods of the League in forming an adequate second line of defence, we are yet pre- pared to admit that so far as it has gone, the League has done useful service in opening the nation's eyes to the inefficiency of our present day complex military system.

Northern Union footballers are now out-and-out , professionals. The saving clause, that relating to the employment of every player, was abolished at the annual meeting of the Union on Wednesday evening. It has long been foreseen that such a step would be forced on the authorities, but the change is none the less an important one and likely to have far-reaching effects. In the first place it means that playets will no longer he ' compelled to work before they play, they may idle all the week and still play football on Saturday—if they keep themselves fit. Actually however, most players in the Union will probably retain their employment, for very few, if any clubs will be able to aff.,rd the entire support of a largo body of professionals and see to their physical training in addition. So far as Warrington club is concerned the men will still have to work to live, but a greater measure of responsibility for their physical fitness will be thrown upon the officials and committee. In all probability the new departure in policy will have the effect of ' confining Rugby football in the north even more exclusively to the large and wealthy clubs, and in this respect it may prove a serious matter in the future, for the rival code is rapidly pushing on one side many of those Rugby organisations which are in a parlous financial state. Whatever the subsequent outcome, however, the Warrington Club will, we believe, be able to keep in the fore- front and participate, as heretofore,in the honours falling to the best exponents of the Rugby game.

Education is the question of the moment at Stockton Heath, and the progress of the negotia- tions between the Parish Council and the Cheshire County Council will be watched with interest by people on this side of the water. The " Heathens practically condemn the scheme all ' round, but the main ground of their objection is that the cost of the new school which the Countir Council propose to erect will, if based on the usual estimate of 15 for each scholar accommodated, total £12,000. The villagers quite naturally con- sider this unnecessary extravagance, and tiler? seems to be no reason why the Council should not be able to devise a less costly scheme. A conference between a sub-com- mittee of the Parish Council and the Church School managers is being held with a view to formulating alternative proposals. Warrington,, people will be most concerned with the "Heathens hall-threat to exclude Warrington children from the Stockton Heath school, on the ground that they make no contriblition to the rates. ProbahlY investigation would prove that the entire afea tr" be served by the new school—Stockton ide,th Latchford Without,and Walton Inferior—sends as many if not more children to receive their educa- tion in Warrington than go from the borough to Stockton Heath.

A MINING ENGINEER'S CLAIM.

Penketh Man's Successful Action.

At the Newnham (Gloucestershire) County Court 01 Friday of last week, his Honour Judge Eiicott, had before him a claim brought by Andrew Jackson, mining engineer and colliery manager and a member of the firm of A. Jackson and Sons, Penketh, mining ad n mechanical engineers, for £lOO balance of commission and salary alleged to be due, from Albert Schofield, colliery proprietor, residing at Nuthurst Farm Fail worth, Manchester. Mr. Herbert Williams, Of Mon- mouth,appeared for the plaintiff,and Mr. Frank Treasure of Gloucester, for the defendant. The claim was for balance of commission in respect of the purchase or Addis Hill Colliery in the Forest of Dean, and salary fle colliery manager at .24 per week, from the 10th Novem- ber last to the 19th April of the present year. Defendant counterclaimed £4 us. 6d. as being due from plaintiff to defendant, this amount had been paid into Court. The plaintiff's contention was that although he re- ceived a week's notice defendant's employ as manager of the Addis Hill Colliery, he was entitled to a month' notice, and so informed the defendant, who, however, had never given such notice to him. The plaintiff wrote to the defendant on the 12t11 November saying that at the end of November he should require one month's notice. He met with an accident at the colliery on the 19th November the date upon which the week's notice expired, but be stated that he went to the colliery as usual until the 23rd November, when he finally left and went home to Penketh, where he had since remained. The defence was that plaintiff was engaged and pai4 as a weekly servant, and that therefore a weeks' notice was the legal one. Alternately, however, the defendant contended that the plaintiff, by his letter of November, 12th, gave notice to leave on December 31st, and that' therefore he would in any case only be entitled te wages as from the 19th November to the 31st December, being six weeks at £4, viz. £24. His Honour held that plaintiff was entitled to recover the whole amount of the salary claimed, and gave judr ment on the claim for £lOO with costs. On the counter claim, he gave judgment for Schofield for £4 us 6ct• without costs.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD. Golf Champion of France. 1 , Liverpool golfers will, says a Liverpool contemporary be delighted to hear that Mr. Arthur H. Crosileld, popular member of the Royal Liverpool Club, on Satur- day won the golf championship of France. Mr. Crosfleld .: met Sir Edgar Vincent, M.P., for Exeter, in the flue! of ' the tournament, and bad an easy victory. Mr. Crosflola has on several occasions taken a team of Hoylake golfers to the Riviera to play against a team got together bY the Grand Duke Michael of Russia, and more than ones the Grand Duke has visited Hoylake as the guest of Mr. Crosfleld. _ _ _

Mr. Crosfleld's enthusiasm for golf and sport getter` ally is well known in Warrington, and our readers generally will, we feel sure, heartily congratulate his' upon his success. The stiffest matches Mr. Crosfield had were in the demi-semi final on Friday morning, when he had to niee Livingston; and in the semi-final on Friday afternoo3- when he met Mallaby Deeley. Where was the Cup? It was conspicuously absent from the Football Club " Smoker " on Thursday 'evening, •* * * The experience that we have had of government by the aristocracy for the classes will hardly conduce to our soon again trying the experiment.—" Truth." He is a much-fancied norapetitor. * * * We notice elsewhere an advertisement for the cure nf dull razors "-My razor tablets " are the stated remedy of the advertiser, but whether they have to be taken internally we do not know. 'Tis possible, almost. The properties which have been compressed into tablets of recent years are remarkable. You can cure indigestion, develop photographs,and sharpen dull razors by tablets ! The Queen had just ascended the throne amid the plaudits of thousands of spectators when a donkey, which had • taken up a convenient position behind the throne, ' joined in the general rejoicings and brayed lustily. Her Majesty must have been impressed by this signal mark of homage..

By holding their revels in Whit-week the villagers have been favoured with the ideal weather coaditions which were denied their neighbours of Knutsford who cling ' to the real " May pay" observance. This particu- lar crab possesses a claw shaped, with wonderful exactness, like a human leg adorned with a high heel *boot. The resemblance rendered is even greater to the human pedal extremity by the fact that the "boot" Portion of the claw is jet black. Nothing like it has ever been seen before by the crab cognoscenti and Mr. Lee is naturally very proud of his unique possession. The controversy on "tainted money," and particu- larly on the acceptance of Mr. Bockefeller's donation to the Board of Foreign Missions, has been acute in -tkrnerica during the past two months, especially in the meetings of Congregational associations. * * * The Whitsuntide vacation would come as a relief to members of Parliament who have been kept in exceed- ingly close attendance to their Parliamentary duties this session up to Whitsuntide. The subjects have been varied in character and the divisions frequent and heavy. In one the extraordinarily large number of 571 members took part. In 168 over 250 voted, in 135 over 300, in sixty-eight more than 400, and in six over 500. There were 199 divisions in all. We notice that Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P.' voted in 102 divisions, Colonel It Pilkington in 117, Colonel W. Hall Walker in 97, and the Hon. Alan do Tatton 'Egerton in 152. * * * The urban and rural authorities in Cheshire are rising in their wrath against the County Council, Which proposes to expend £154,000 upon new schools. Education is rapidly becoming too expensive for the members of the local authorities, and they are going to combine in order to hold a conference which may have some weight with the County Council. Mr. Stokes 'of the Runcorn Rural Council advanced a new idea in discussing the matter. "He did not kee the benefits Which arose from education. Amongst the youth of the present day there was a disinclination for work Which they did not see in former times." This may be rather old-fashioned of Mr. Stokes, but does over- 'educating lessen the desire for work? There is a nice problem here for enthusiastic educationists. * * * It is dangerous to form hasty conclusions, and on , 'Tuesday Mr. S. Woodward, a prominent member of the Runcorn Board of Guardians, had a special oppor- • thnity of realizing the fact. A letter had been received protesting against any new authority being formed to relieve the unemployed. Mr. Woodward Placed an exactly different construction on the letter, and with the remark that there were already Sufficient public bodies, moved that the communication should un the table. This motion was seconded, and the Chairman was about to put it to the meeting when the Clerk explained' the true purport of the letter. "Oh then I move that we strongly support it," said Mr. Woodward with alacrity, and the Board once more hastened to follow his lead, with the result that the letter, rescued in the nick of time from the ignominy "lying on the table" has now the Board's most Cordial approval.

Woinen's Liberal Association Picnic.,

The annual picnic of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association is this year destined for Belle Vue. Monday, June 26th, has been fixed for the date and it is expected that the party will consist of from twelve hundred to fifteen hundred people. Men's tickets will be 3s. including railway fares admission to Belle Vue, and tea. The tickets may be had from Mr. J. Howes, jun., at the Liberal Club.
