Fire at Castle Rubber Works. , Some excitement was created on Monday morning when the Fire Brigade was summoned to an outbreak at the Castle Rubber Works. A good muster of men under Superintendent Turner and Deputy Superinten- dent Turner,was quickly on the scene with a steam fire engine. The fire had originated in the boiler room were some repairs were in progress. The artificers had been using a naked light to aid them in the work and this, it appears ignited some parafin. Fortunately the brigade had no difficulty in extinguishing the flames before they had time to spread. Had the fire extended to the rubber department the consequences would have been serious. As it was, the damage was ve-y slight. An employe named Fitzgerald, who was working at the boiler, sustained injuries to the hand and ankle.

Chat Moss Ablaze.

On'llonday night the Leigh Fire Brigade was sum- moned to what proved to be the largest fire over seen on Chat Moss. The three years' stock of moss litter stacks, belonging to the Griendts—een Moss Litter Company, near Astley station, was ablaze. The sheet of flame, four hundred yards long, was fanned by a strong easterly wind, and spread rapidly, acres of bracken also catching, fire. Owing to the engine sinking repeatedly in the moss it was two hours before the brigade was able to commence preventing the flames extending to the adjoining stacks. Despite the exertions of the brigade, who obtained water for their steamers from a pond four hundred yard away, the flames spead to two other rows of peat stacks, each a quarter of a mile long, forming three gigantic fires, visible many miles away. The spectacle was watched by crowds of pe pie. The fire- men remained all night, endeavouring to save other stacks. While leaping across a ditch near the fire George Yarw md, aged fourteen, of Chapel-street, Leigh, fractured his leg.

ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS MINERAL WATERS.—Advt. 1 Exhibition of Gas Cookers. As time goes on, the use of coal gas for cooking purposes is becoming increasingly popular. House- wives have not been slow to recognize the valoe of a fuel which on the grounds of economy and suitability, has so much to recommend it, and during the summer months in particular many thousands of gas cookers are in use. Messrs. Fletcher Russell and C 0.,, Ltd., who possess a high reputation as manufacturers of all kinds of gas cooking appliances, have this week been holding an exhibition of some of their lead- ing lines in the Parish Room, Grappenhall-road, Latchford. Commencing on Tuesday afternoon, there have been large numbers of visitors to the exhibition each day, the course of cookery lectures delivered in the afternoons and evenings, by Mrs. H. M. Young (diplomee, Liverpool, London and Paris) constituting a special attraction. The exhibits, attractively arranged round the room, suffice to give one an excellent idea of the variety of goods which the employs are daily engaged in producing. Special attention has been called to the treble-cased indestructible cooking ranges, the great advantage being that the patent porcelain enamel linings can be taken out by any servant, cleaned and replaced without trouble, and without removing a screw. They are unlocked by simply sliding the top plate forward. So quick are these ovens in heating that the lightest puff pastry, if placed in the cold oven and the gas then lighted, will rise and bake as per- fectly as if plac,-dit once in the hottest oven. The I other exhibits include gas fires, stoves, and washing boilers and there is a very creditable display of incandescent gas lighting, pendants, brackets, &c. It may be mentioned that the firm's cookers can be hired or obtained on the five years' hire purchase system from the Warrington Corporation gas department at low half-yearly rentages. They are fixed free up to 35 feet of piping. Mr. J. Knowles has had charge of the exhibition. The exhibition will be continued at the Hall Caf4, Victoria-square, Stockton Heath next week when it will be open from Monday to Friday. Lectures on cooking will be delivered by Msg. Young.

CELEBRATING THE VICTORY

FOOTBALL CLUB SMOKING CONCERT.

Speech by Mr. A. H. Crosfield.

ALDERMAN TINNION'S DEFENCE OF FOOTBALL.

The success of the Warrington Football Club in winning the Northern Union Cup has been fittingly recognized by the townspeople: The concluding stages of the celebrations were reached on Thursday evening when the members of the club held a smoking concert in the Parr Hall, the large attendance afford- ing evidence of the active interest which is taken in the welfare of the club. The proceedings were marked with great enthusiasm. which promises well for the continued prosperity of the club. Occasion was 'taken to present the players with the medals which they had won. The medals presented to the first team were of gold with the colours of the three counties, Lancashire, Yorkshire and Cheshire, in true heraldic style. At the back were the names of the recipient and the words" Winners,l9o4-5 W.R.F.C." The combination medals were also of gold with the words "Lancashire Rugby Combination," and a coat of arms, with the inscription "Season 19045 W.R.F.C." :qr. W. R. Neil, the popular president occupied the chair and was supported by Mr. A. IL Crasfield, Aldermen Pierpoint and Tinnion, Mr. T. Pemberton (hon. treasurer), Mr. H. Ashton (hon. secretary), Mr. 1. E. Warren, and members of the committee.

F On the proposition of the CtIAIRMIN the custornar,y loyal toasts were drunk with nlit.sical honours. PRESENTATION TO' MR. W. R. NEIL.

Mr. H. ASHTON' then performed the pleasing duty of I presenting a gold medal to the President as a memento of the team's success. Not only was the medal a recog- nition of Mr. Neil's able services, but, Mr. Ashton said, they hoped it would in years to come recall pleasant recollections of the days when the Warrington Club earned a little distinction and brought some credit to the town. (Applause.) It was certainly an honour for Mr. Neil to have been president of the club during that successful period, and they hoped he would recall the time with pride. He (Mr. Ashton) would like to take the opportunity of congratulating Mr. Arthur Crosaeld upon having achieved the most honourable distinction of golf champion of France. (Applause.) Mr. Oros- field's performance certainly reflected credit upon the good old town and they hoped before long Mr. Crosffeld would, by winning the English Amateur Golf Champion- ship attain the dream of his life. (Applause). Mr. NEIL, in accepting the present, said he considered it a very great honour to hold such a memento of the Warrington club's biggest victory up to the present time. He was very proud when on two occasions War- rington won the South-West Lancashire cup, and also when they twice fought their way to the final, but he could hardly express his feelings when the Warrington team returned on that recent memorable night, bringing home the Northern Union cup. (Applause). He since`rely thanked the club for that little memento, not only for its value, but more so for the associations which it recalled. He hoped this would no; be the last time by a- long way that Wirringtoa would win the cup. (Applause). MEDALS PRESENTED TO THE FIRST TEAM.

Mr. NEIL then presented the Norther a Union modals to the members of the first team, and said he also intended to make each player a present of a photograph of the team.

Responding on behalf of the team Mr. J. Hi',tam said they were very proud to receive the medals. They had had hard lines on two occasion and he had two runners-up medals, but he was pleased that now he had a first class medal. (Applause). He hoped the club would be able to repeat their achievement. (Applause.) He would like to second Mr. Ashton's remarks about Mr. Crostield winning the golf championship bf France. (Hear, hear.) He was pleased that they had a thorough gentleman who took an interest in sport and could win a championship, as well as working lads who could win the Northern Union Cup. (Applause.)

GIFT TO THE CLUB.

Mr. NV. R. NBtr, then asked the club's acceptance of a handsomely-framed photograph of the team, officials, and cup. to be hung in the committee room.

ALDERMAN PIERPOINT PROPOSES "THE CLUB." Alderman W. PIERPOINT proposed the toast of "The Club." He had always said that since the very incep- tion of the club—and that went back many years—the team had always played good football on every occasion, with perhaps the exception of the meeting with Batley at Leeds. They did not play as good football then as they ought to have done, or they would now have been commemorating their aecond victory in the final. (Ap- plause.) He had the pleasure of seeing the team meet Hull Kingston Rovers at Leeds. They came on in their old style, just as if they were playing at WilJerspool, and he felt sure after the first five minutes' play that the cup was corning to Warrington, (Applause). The Warrington Club, whatever might be said about it,was a good club in every sense of the word. (Applause.) It had not only a long roll of members, but it included many very skilful football players, and be believed that taking it as a body it was composed of jolly good fellows. (Hear, hear and laughter.) If they only went on the lines that they had been adopting—playing the, game in the manner they played it at Leeds,—there was no doubt thatthey would be worthy of every commenda- tion and of the heartiest support. They hada good captain who was supported by good players ; an excellent presi- dent; a good committee, while a better body of sup- porters never rallied round any football club. ( Applause) He was Sara they would do their best to maintain the great reputation they had in the North of England, and he wished the club every success. (Applause).

MEDALS F'OR Tag " "TE ANL ALDERMAN TINNION DEFENDS FoorBALL. THE RECENT' CELEBRATIONS. Before presenting the Lancashire Combination medals to the "A' team,Alderrnan Tinnion saidihe was sure that proud as they were of the first team winning the Northern Union Cup, they were equally prowl—personally he was prouder—of the" A" team winning the combination medals. lie did not wish in any way to detract from the excellent results achieved by the first tearn,but consider- ing the many times they had to'play,the great number of clubs they met, and the splendid results, the " A team's record excelled anything that had been accom- plished by any football team in Warrington. (Applause.) lie was one of the oldest members of the club, having been associated with it for over twenty-five years, and he could speak with some authority of the value and advantages of football. (Applause). It seemed neces- sary, even in Warrington at the present time, that it should be pointed out that it was a good thing to honour Warrington teams when they had accomplished such feats as their teams had performed during the season. (Applause). Some men_ had lad the folly to make a very serious complaint because the Mayor and Cor- poration gave the people of Warrington an opportunity of showing their appreciation of what the teams had done. (Applause). When those individuals came to consider their action, he believed they would regret it. It also seemed necessary—as there were so many people to find fault with—that something should be said in defence of football. As a teetotaler he said that the game of football played on Saturday afternoons did a great deal to provide amusement of a healthy, rational, sensible kind and keep the workingmen and many more away from sornethin. which might be a great deal worse. (Applause). 'Alluding to the style of play of the Warrington club, Alderman Tinnion said he was heartily sorry that such tinkering had gone on in the Northern Union during the last few years, and seemed again to be taking a front place, for in his opinion it would do a great deal of harm to Northern Union football. If the Union ComFaittee would instil into the other clubs the desirability and advantage of playing the same style of football as Warrington played there would be no need to alter rules. (Applause.) When their opponents gave them anything like a chance, the Warrington team played an open and inter- esting game which the spectators liked to see. (Hear, hear.) If other clubs would do the same, Northern Union football v-ould be much more popular. In many centres where Rugby football had had tho lead the Association authorities were attempting to supplant it by fostering association teams. He did not Say that in Warrington there was not room for the two kinds of football, in fact he believed there was. but they must be very careful, and so must the Northern Union, lest in their tinkering with rules and style of play they did h great harm to the Rugby game and helped to bring into more prominence tho Association code. 'Applause.) Alderman Tinnion proceeded to congratulate the " A "team upon their admirable record of 27 matches played, 24 won and three lost. The team played good football and he was con- vinced that that was the reason they had gained the championship. (Applause). A GRIEVANCE.

He thought it would do Northern Union football a great deal of good if the Press would extend fair play to Warrington and bring into more prominence the style of play adopted by Warrington. at the same time recommending other clubs in the Union to adopt it. In that event he felt sure they need have no fear for the ultimate success of Northern Union football. (Applause. LYMM MAY QUEEN. ____ • Pleasing Pageant. Lymm was f fee en Thursday. On every hand were. indications that the inhabitants of the district were taking a keen and active interest in the festivities attending the annual crowning of the ;•.May Queen. Flags fluttered gaily in the tbre,eze and children laughed gleefully in the eager anticipation of the enjoyments of what is to them the principal day of titer - year. The probable state of the weather occasioned some anxiety but the fears aroused by the heavy showers of the previous day happily pr wed groundless and the day's pro- gramme was carried:out for the greater part in brilliant sunshine. Occasionally the sky was overcast , but nothing short of a heavy downpour of rain would have put the damper on the proceedings, so completely did the children and their elders give themselves up to the pleasures of the day. It is needless to enlarge up m the natural beauties of the old world village which are well known to Warrington,people, suffice it to say that seldom has the village looked more attractive. Naturally the district was invaded by persons desirous of witnessing the celebrations, and towards 1.30 a crowd of some thousands congregated near the cross, in days long past the scene of punishmnnt of wrongdoers, but which on Thursday formed the starting place of the pro- cession always associated with the Whitsuntide rejoic- ings. Fairly punctually the children were marshalled in their proper order, and to the accompaniment of the bands the National Anthem was heartily sung. Imme- diately afterwards the Lymtu and Oughtrington Band struck up a lively air, and the procession moved off headed by the scholars of the Congregational Sunday school. A number of small but exceptionally happy little children were accommodated on a gaily decorated lurry. Sixteen little lads, described in the official programme as vam- pires, but looking more like elves next made their appearance. The British Empire was represented by a number of lads attired in the costumes peculiar to the different nationalities under the Union Jack. Of I decorated lurries there were quite a number, and the procession would not be complete with the caricature of the shoe in)which the aged dame of the fable took lup her residence. On Thursday the old lady was con- spicuons by her absence the shoe being inhabited by some half-a-dozen juveniles whose smiling faces just appeared over the edge of their novel carriage. The dandy of the tenth century associated with men of every century up to the present day. May- pole dancers were of course included and in addition there were twelve garland dancers bedecked with roses. The Duchess of Devonshire was accompanied by Dolly Varden while Mary Queen of Scots and Joan of Arc forgot the troubles of their lives as they with the others walked gaily round the village. Three diminative policemen were present to restore order if any of the arrivals which were present threatened to disagree, while a like number of equally small soldiers afforded a sense of security. It was fitting that" night" should travel with "Egypt " and that the Yachting girl should go in company with the " Union Jack." Carmen and a rouch girl chattered and joked with Portia while an archer and a gondolier followed in the wake of the King's Jester. Interest was occasioned by the appearance of the band of the training ship" Indefatigable" which discoursed suit- able music along the route. No disrespect is intended for their Majesties the Queens in referring to them last of all. Both conducted then:selves with proper queenly dignity and Oar:eft:lly bowed their acknowledgements of the homage of their subjects for the day. The May Queen (Miss Alice White) looked every inch a queen in a dress of white cashmere trimmed with applique lace, and a crimson. robe trimmed with ermine. She carried a shower bouquet of sweet peas and roses. Her maids of honour M. Bate, A. Green L. Astbary, L. Read, E. Hurst, and N. Taylor wore dresses of cream nun's veiling and wreaths. The crown bearer was J. Read, sword bearer A. Skel:iorn, and sceptre bearer N. Thornicroft. The train bearers were W. Jackson, and F. Read. The Rose Queen (Miss M. Bradbury) was similarly attired to her sharer of the honours of the day except that her robe was of pale pink. Her maids of honbur were E. Thomas and M. Whitelegg Having completed the journey round the village an adjournment was made to a large field where the May Queen was crowned by Mrs. J. Hardy Holt, Lymm, and she was invested with her sceptre. Both Queens were presented with a copy of Tennyson's poems. The ceremony concluded the day given up to sports, an .excellent programme opening with the successful plaiting of the maypole. Rifle and cutlass drills were given by the "Indefatigable" boys. The "Vampire" gave an exhibi- tion of their terpischoran art and a garland dance was also included in the programme. The programme was repeated in the evening when in addition the bands played for dancing which was kept up until dusk when ended one of the most successful May Day festivals in connection with the village. Praise is due to the officials for their admirable arrangements to ensure the success of the day's proceed- ings. They were :—Chairman, Mr. C. Raper ; Vice- Chairman, Mr. R. H. Gotobed and Mr. W. G. Groves ; M.C. Mr. F. P. Cook; Hon. Treasurer, Mr. R. H. Gotebed ; Hon. Secretary, Mr. James D. 13. Smith ; Assistant Hon. Secretary, Mr. W. G. Porter. Ladies' Committee: Presidents, Mrs. R. H. Gotobed and Mrs. J. H. Holt ; Hon. Secretary, Miss A. Roden ; Mesdames P. Ardern, Cooke, Groves, Leather, Porter, Simpson, Misses S. E. Boond, Diamond, L. Diamond, Heesorn, Hesketh, E. A WORD TO THE WISE.

The fickle nature of the climate of this country often gives rise to a variety of ailments and complaints, which assuming at first the form of only a slight indisposition. if neglected and unheeded may become the germ of serious diseases, which will nndermine the constitution, and finally result in a long and lingering illness, and perhaps terminate fatally. The variable temperature and the changeable weather of the last month or so, makes it incumbent on all of us to exercise wise and prudent precautions to counteract the evil effects which the weather may have had upon our health and comfort. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

ALLEGED ENBEULEMENT AT STOCKTON HEATH.

Some two months ago a warrant was granted by the magistrates sitting at the Daresbury Petty Sessions against a man named Kitchen for embezzling £1 38.10 d. the moneys of his employers, Messrs. R. White and Sons, mineral water manufacturers. At the sessions on Tuesday Mr. A. Browne said the police had bad the warrant in hand from that time, and had done their best to trace the man. It was pointed out at the last sessions by Mr. Greenall (the magistrates' clerk) that Kitchen was in receipt of an army pension, and he would lose this if a conviction ensued. He (Mr. Browne) was now in- structed by his clients to say that Kitchen's mother had approached them, and they thought-that for him to lose his pension of 10s. 6d. a week for life would be rather too severe a punishment for the crime, especially as he was only a young man, and had served in the South African war. Their soft heart had been touched, and on their behalf he now asked the Bench to alloy the warrant to be withdrawn —The magistrates agreed to the course suggested by Mr. B^ONVIIII.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
FURIOUS MOTORLNd AT PRESTON BROOK.

At Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, before Mr. John White and other magistrates, John W. Catchpole, of 163, Moscow Drive, Stonycrort, Liverpool, was sum- moned for driving a motor cycle to the danger of the public at Preston Brook on May 6th. He pleaded not guilty. Sergeant Morgan said that on the Saturday afternoon in question he was at Preston Brook for the purpose of timing motors over a measured distance Defendant was driving a motorcycle and covered 220 yards in 21 seconds, being thus travelling at 21 miles an hour. The road at this point was most dangerous and in addition there were children playing there at the time. Defendant stopped when ordered by Constable Sumners. In reply to the defendant, the officer paid he did pull up in about 20 yards when requpsted. Defend- ant said he did not have to apply his brakes to pull up and he did not consider he was driving to the public danger, as dere was no traffic on the road at the time. He was fined 20s. and costs, his Hoene* being also en- dorsed.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLEGED THEFT OF A HORSE.

At the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Joseph Marsh, of Ishintwich, was charged with stealing a horse, of the value £2, the property of George Kinsey, farmer, Lightwood Farm, Dutton.—Prisoner was repre- sented by Mr. .1. P. Whittingham, of Nantwich, and pleaded not guilty.—Superintendent Bunion stated that Mr. Kinsey had a black mare he wished to dispose of and told his man Kelly that if a man from Warrington (a prospective purchaser) came, he was to be allowed to see the horse. Prisoner came, and made a bargain to buy the horse for £4. He paid Kelly £1 Is., and saying be would pay the rest to Kinsey, took the horse away. Nothing more was heard of him and the matter was reported to the police.—When Kelly gave evidence to making the bargain with the prisoner, Mr. Whitt Ingham contended that it showed a sale and therefore there could be no theft.—The Magistrates' Clerk held that this was so, and the case was thereupon dismissed, Mr. Whittingham remarking that the horse bad been returned.

RECKLESS MOTOR DRIVER FINEP.-LAD rNJURED AT

%VARRINGT6N.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Tuesday, before Dr. Fox and other magistrates, Richard David Jones, motor chauffeur, Halton Common, Halton, was summoned for driving a motor car along Sankey-street, on June 2nd, at a speed dangerous to the public. Defendant pleaded not guilty.—Superintendent Monaghan said the defendant, in company with another man, was seen in a motor car coming from the direction of Bank Quay Bridge at from twenty to thirty miles an hour which was a very dangerous speed. When near the Post Office, the car knocked a boy down named William Jenks and he had to be taken to a doctor. Frederick Holding, motor engineer, said he was talking to a client inside "Messrs. Bellian mid. Davies' shop . . ..

when he saw the defendant drive past. He remarked to the client that if he knew the number of the car, he would have reported the defendant for going at such a , rate. When the car had reached the Post Office, it knocked a little boy down, he ran out to the defendant rnd told the defendant he would report him. Witness had had seven years experience and the car would be going between 25 and 30 miles an hour. There would be between 50 and 60 children in the street at the time. Richard Thomas, labourer, Longshaw-street, said he was sitting on a seat nearly opposite Messrs. Bellian and Davies's shop when be saw the car going in the direc- tion of the Post Oilice. He could hardly see how many people were in the car as it was going at a very fast speed—about 25 or 30 miles an hour.He saw the car knock the boy down. —Ernest Unsworth, Knutsford-road, said he was pass-

_ log along Sankey-street when he saw the car going at a considerable speed—l 6 to 18 miles an hour. He thought defendant would have to he very careful to avoid an accident. The car was on the wrong side of the road. —William Jenks said he was crossing the road to go through the Park when he heard the horn of a motor ,car. He turned back again aud the car knocked him down.—ln answer to the Chairman, witness said he did not think the accident would have happened if he bad not turned back. The car caught him and cut his head, pushing him a short iistance.—John Gray, postman, called by the defendant, said Jones' car was going at about ten miles an hour. It was three yards from the left side of the road and the driver turned to the right on seeing the boy. The boy turned back with the result that he was knocked down. He picked the lad up and took him to a doctor's. Islo accident would have happened if the boy had not hesitated and turned back.—John Johnson, the owner of the car, said defendant was driv- ing at about eight miles an hour. There were several boys in the road and if Jenks had not been deaf, the accident would not have happened. The other boys ran out of the way of the car. If the car had been going at the rate mentioned by one of the witnesses, the boy would have been killed. It was not going more than three miles an hour when the accident happened. There was no negligcnce at all on the driver's part.—The magistrates retired, and, on returning into Court, the Chairman said the ear had been driven at a speed dangerous to the public and defendant would be fined 20s. and costs (22 17s.Cd. in all) and his licence endorsed

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

County Council Expenditure. •

PROTEST TO BE MADE.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present—Messrs. S. Woodward .(presiding) A. Greenway, J. Bolshaw, H. Eyes, G. Egerton Warburton, J. Stokes. J. Whitlow, T. Baxter, H. M. Davies, C. E. Linaker, J. H. Davies, J. Smith, T. Wright, T.Guest,W. Harrison, G. Pilling, S. Horton, J. L. Jones, E. Bretherton, J. Spruce, J. Hallows, J. Whitley, R. Billington, T. Antwis, Admiral Jones Parry,Messrs.G.F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Swinton and W. Diggle (surveyors), J. Farrington (inspector), and Dr. Adams (medical officer.)

===== 0002601_19050617_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNTY_ COUNCIL EXPENDITURE:

A letter was read from the Ncuthwich Urban Council calling attention to the increase in the County Council's expenditure, and suggesting a conference of Urban and Rural Councils with a view to the forwarding of a strong representation to the County Council on the question. In the event of the suggestion being disapproved the Council asked the Runcorn Council to forward a resolu- tion to the County Council on the subject. Mr. Linaker thought it was time the ratepayers should protest against the excessive expenditure of the County Council. The increase was caused by the administration , of the Education Act but for the life of him he could not see where the benefit came in under the present I system. Much more money was being spent than was I spent byll the old School Boards which earned the I highest possible grants and got the best possible ! reports. The County Council contemplated the , spending of £154,000 upon new schools which would: cost something like £l5 per head. That sum was exces- sive and extravagant. New schools had been build at a I cost of from £8 to £9 per head and he thought they ; should protest against the spending of Zlri per head to gratify the vanity of the Education Committee who ! wanted to be able to say "See what beautiful schools we have built." It was time the ratepayers made them-I selves heard. He moved that the Council combine with the Northwich Council with a view to the holding of a con ference. Mr. Antwis seconded.

Mr. Linaker pointed out that at Helsby it was pro- posed to build a school which would cost £3,000 of which the Helsby people would be asked to pay £2,000. He did not say the existing schools in that district were models but they were earning the highest possible grants and the County Council shonld defer the spend- ing of the money until times improved. Admiral Jones Parry said he knew of an instance where the County Council had gone out of their way to spend money. A short time ago they sent four splendidly hound books upon the teaching of conduct to children. Those books would take a month to read, and must have cost a great deal of money. The school managers at Grappenhall had set a good example and declined to read the books. He could give another instance of extravagance. The school inspector visited a certain school and ordered the erection of wash bowls in the school. His instructions were carried out. Six months later another inspector was appointed, and on visiting those particular schools said the wash bowls had been put in altogether the wrong place, and must be taken out.

Mr. Hallows said the County Council proposed to build schools at Stockton Heath at a cost of £12,000. It was a great deal too much to pay. If the schools were built at a reasonable cost he did not think anyone would object. Mr. Stokes said he sympathised with the ratepayers. The County Council were quite aware of the complaints which were being made with regard to excessive expen- diture, and at their last meeting were discussing how they could economise. Various suggestions were made, but the principal discussion was with regard to the attendance at school of children under five years of age. It was realized that if they could &et the law altered to prevent those children attending school it would prevent the spending of thousands of pounds, and would relieve a great deal of the overcrowding. If they were in business and found that their premises were too small, he did not think they would build new premises, but that they would enlarge the existing buildings to meet with the requirements of the times. Thesame thing applied to the schools which he thought should be made large enough to pro- vide the required accommodation and so built that they could be enlarged as occasion demanded. He did not see the benefits which arose from education. Amongst the youth of the present day there was a disinclination for work which they did not see in former times. Mr. Linaker's motion was carriedandMessrs.Linaker, Pilling, and Admiral Jones Parry were appointed the deputation to attend the conference if one was arranged

===== 0002601_19050617_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WATERING OF THE ROADS.

The Clerk reported the receipt of a resolution from the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee asking the District Council to take into immediate consideration the question of the periodical watering of the district roads.

Mr. Whitley said the question was before the Council some time ago and referred back. With the return of the hot dry weather the nuisance caused by the dust had become intolerable and the inhabitants of the district were tip in arms against it. The filth and the dust which was blown into the grocers and butchers shops had caused a great number of complaints to be made. It was high time something should be done with regard to the watering of the streets and he moved that the Council take the question into their immediate consideration. Mr. Hallows seconded. The people of Stockton Heath were like the Irish and Home Rule, they wanted a little water. _

Mr. Stokes said the question not only applied to Stockton Heath, but to the whole of the district. It was decided to refer the matter to the highways committee.

THE REGULATION OF MOTOR TRAFFIC. .. .

The Clerk read a letter from the Northwich Rural District Council suggesting the holding of a conference of Cheshire Rural and Urban Councils for the purpose of considering motor traflie and the effect of such traffic on the road with a view to the framing of a resolution petitioning the Local Government Board and the County Council on the matter. It was decided to support the action of the North- wich Council, Messrs. Woodward, Whitley, and Linaker being elected to form the deputation. The Ramsey Rural district Council wrote expresping themselves in favour of an amendment of the law in relation to heavy motor traffic, and suggesting that the District Council would enter into a contract with the drivers of heavy engines for the use of the District roads, and that if terms were not arranged to order that heavy motor traffic should be confined to the main roads. The resolution would not apply to threshing machines and other engines used for agricultural purposes. The matter was referred to the Parliamentary Com- mittee.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TREATMENT._ OF SMALLPDX OUTBREAKS,

The Clerk read a letter from the Runcorn Urban Council with reference to theapplication of the Council to borrow money for the purpose of extending the infectious diseases hospital. The Local Government Board would only sanction the loan on condition that no smallpox patients were treated at the present hospital. The Council desired to know whether the District Council would be prepared to join with them in the erection of a smallpox hospital. The Clerk explained that the District Council already bad a smallpox hospital. He thought they might enter into some arrangement whereby the Runcorn Urban Council could use their smallpox hospital. The matter was referred to the Smallpox Hospital Committee.

It was decided to accept the tender of Mr. Ralph Johnson for the furnishing of the infections diseases hospital at the cost of £306.

TWO KILLED BY LIGHTNING.

The greater part of South Wales experienced thunder- storms on Wednesday, and at Newcastle Emlyn and Carmarthen there was a very severe storm, during which John Davies, a farmer, of Glancock, Miedvv, whilst at dinner with his family, was killed by a flash of lightning which entered at the chimney of the farmhouse.— During a thunderstorm at Ennis on Tuesday night a girl named Maloney was killed by lightning.

Ulu. Moan Ricinsvay.)

A MODERN SIREN,

ROBERT BARR (Author of " The Countess Telda." V.)

As the physician, friend, and distant relative of ; the late IBM Marjory Sedgwick, I set down in 1 narrative form a connected series of extracts from the documents she left in my care, believing such publication to be in, accord with her wishes. I do this with some hesitation, not being rims- ; timed to write for the public prints, while the ' tenets of my profession point towards secrecy with regard to communications rather than the reverse. But in what was doubtless the last note Miss Sedgwiek wrote, she left these letters in my care, with the injunction to use them should a biography of Professor Wallis Dent ever be writ- ten, so that the record of his final days upon earth might be given from his own pen. In case no biography appeared, she wished in edited selec- tion from Professor Dent's communications to be printed in some suitable magazine or review, and thltM, its it were, the material should be put in evidence for future use. So far as rain aware the life of Profensor Dent has never been written. It is probable that at the time of his death ho WWI too young a man to have yet made his mark in tlie world' 'cit scientific research, which doubtless would have been the case hud he lived. LIM one hook on the legends of Southern Italy, shows groat research in the literary works of the past, with patient and intelligent investigation of such obtitillitloB as bore alma his sul jeet, but the yol- lune MIR rather a promise ef the future, than a: guarantee of lasting fame already accomplished. Naturally to the woman who loved him, ho Ind Areal}, all wined. that eminence which would ul- timately have been his, hut this flattering esti- mate is not shared by tho mole coldly critical Pwient ill° world.

: Miss teedgwiek was a shy, retiring girl, wile eared little for the social geieties of New York. She had peid several visits to Europe, and lict chief delight was in travel and in reading, the bookm_elie preferred being mainly historical and geogicaphicel. I think, but am not sure' that it was on cue of her Italian journeys she first met Professor Weals Dent, a man some eight or nine years her senior. Be that AS it may, Professor Dent was a guest at her fether's house when he came to America about a year before his death, and it wag known to a few intimate hiends that they were betrothed, although no public an- nouncement of the engagement was ever made. Many of the mensationet statements put forth by . the daily press of New York at the time of the first or the second tragedy were either inaccurate or misleading. It is not true that over study had impaired her intellect and weakened her body. It is not true that the buddenness with which the fact of her lover's death was announced to her while she was rending a letter from him sent her into uneontrolinble frenzy, and of course the de- tailed acoouut of her euicide is only mythical, for the action bad no witnesses. The calamity which had overtaken Professor Dent might have been more tactfully broken to her, but she nevertheless received the blow with that quiet calm, which was habitual to her. Her father and herself ; were seated at the breakfast table. Half a dozen foreign postal cards from Professor Dent had come for her, and she had assorted theni in their order, and was reading the first when her father, glancing over his morning paper, with an ex- clamation of dismay, suddenly and unthinkingly blurted tout the gist a a telegram that Professor Dent had been killed. She turned pale to the lips, and sat there without a word, staring at her father who now tried to soften a statement which could not be mitigated. She asked in a low voice I to see the newspaper, and she read the cablegram ' as well as she could through the tears that, in spite of her marvellous self-control, welled into her eyes; then she rose and went to her room with no exclamation that indicated mental de- rangement. That her mind undoubtedly became clouded I do not intend to deny, but in my opinion this was caused by the ever recurring at- rival of postal cards from her lover, which ex- I tended for a period of nearly fifteen days after she : knew that he was no longer in life. This ghast- ly correspondence, the more terrible because the writing was in a tone of jubilant confidence, with ;11l the joaularity of youth, unfolded itself day by day like a serial story, the tragic end of which is a'-eady known. Postal communication between Italy and America is tardy, a letter sometimes taking from 18 to 20 days, coming by way of England 'Or France, or by direct steamer not much quicker, and thus the series was long drawn out, each missive a mental stab, not to be warded off. When I learned what was going on, I endeavoured to have the postal cards inter- cepted, but she would not have it so, and fearing worse complications, if she were thwarted, I was reluctantly compelled to forego my prohibition. The day I was called in, her friends had become :alarmed. The living writing appeared to have the effect of making her forget momentarily that t the band which penned it was dead, yet some- where in her troubled brain there always floated the shadow of impending disaster, and, flickering through this shadow like vivid forked lightning ; athwart a thunder demi, spasmodic futile effort to avert the threntenieg danger. These alter- nating currents of powerful conflicting emotions, I impinging on a seneitive mind, delicately poised, undoubtedly had the effect of overturning it, act- ing on the brain in a manner somewhat similar to that of n nop volt alternating current of elec- tricity upon the body, blit less mercifully, be- cause instant death was not the result.

On the day I was summoned, a dozen postal cards had arrived, and Marjory sat, very quietly in her boudoir reading them, a lady companion being in the room with her, the same who a few weeks later accompanied her to Europe. Sudden- ly Miss Sedgwick sprang to her feet, the cards Buttering like leaves to the ground. " A tele- graph office ! Where is the telegraph office P I must warn him I" she cried, and, before the other could prevent her, fled dishevelled into the street. There was instant panic in the household, but the girl returned of her own accord after a htief absence. The fact that even the swiftness of lightning coiplil carry ,no warning in tune, came to her before she had gone far, and the hopelesseess of the situation brought her back in unavailing tears. I was telephoned for, but was helpless, for the one suggestion I did make about inierceptitig the postal cards promised a return of the frenzy, iind she was not in it state to be reasoned with. How brave is the man who writes a humorous letter to a friend, for he can- not know in what grim circumstances it may ar- rive ! The careless joking tone of many of these mi.sives strike me even now, in editing them, as gruesomely incongruous ; like the fixed grin of the skeleton standing in the corner of my room. At last they ceased to come, and the eternal silence begun. For a week or inure there was an „ tinexpressed fear in the hottmeheld, that a belated ~,rd would fall in, but it tiever came. Mar- jory herself seemed to expect it, for when :her letters arrived she tossed them over and would look up, saying anxiously, "Is there mithing else f" Finally, she became silently melancholy, and from this state could not be aroused. She gave no trouble to her friends, 311 id was perfectly itineuable to their suggestions, but evidently kept trooding over the event that had blighted her life, the tragedy being indelibly printed en her mind as an imago is tatooed on the body by innumerable stinging pin pricks. When, some three months after the death of Dent; site expressed a desire to go to Europe, the intimation was eagerly agreed to by her father, who thought the sea voyage and the following amigo of scene- would do her good, but I had grave doubts of this, for iihe wished to visit the arena of the tragedy, and the thought of her suicide from the cliff where the professor had fallen, occurred to Inc. The worst came, but not exactly as I had anticipated it. She never saw the Island of Capri. At Gibraltar, she alone of all the passengers, refused to laud, but stood leaning on the rail, gazing at the tall rock. During the voyage she had kept entirely to her- self, never speaking even to her companion, ex- cept when directly addressed. It was night when the steamer left Gibraltar for Naples. The lady who was with her found her writing in their state room, and did not disturb her. That was the last ever seen of Marjory Sedgwick. There seems tin Tolo.no to fintibt that. site Slipned Quietly overboard, white the other passengers were at dinner. •

The fateful pack of cards left in my care were fifty-two in number. On some days the writer had sent as many as ten. None of them were dated except in so far U 8 the post mark was con- cerned, but they were numbered consecutively from one to fifty-one. The tifly-second card bad evidently la en prepared by Miss Sedgwick her- self. It was of the .same size as the others, and on it was pasted the newspaper extract announc- ing Professor Dent 'S deuti. I begin the nam tive with that extract. I have eliminated from the Professor's writing nearly all that is hunior• ous or not bearimr 011 the story lie. haf3 to tell. It

J$ MUMS CO Dotetli t I k 1,4 ri r,r, neseingt, bt gives a hint of the r- iiie that was the direct cause of his death, lilthungli un usricion of this seems ever to have occurred to him. At the end I shall add a note uivix.;; the result of my own ns in Capri, wrionii 1 think will afford an explanation of That was a mvstery to him.

Naples, Dec. 13. A despatch from the Asso- ciated Press continua the report that Professor Dent, of Csmbridge, England, was killed yes- terday in Capri. It is supposed that Professor Dent was making a geological o,xamination of the southern cliffs ofithis island. The rope by which he let himself down the face of the precipice was severed by a sharp rook, and the unfortunate man fell a • distata. of neaey a thousand feet. Death was instantaneous.

"I have arrived once ,more in lovely Capri, truly the isle of the Siren far me, 41$ it was here I first met --but yea remember —I shall rhapso- dize on that in a later letter.' This is a post card and must be dotisidered as such. I expected to come here by Way of Malta,hat the tramp steamer, erratic in its movements, sailed into Naples and will go to Malta later. This was di,mstretta in so far as my Calculations were concerned, for Malta is the cheapest and best island in the world ivhere one may buy Wilkey°, while Capri is the dearest and 'worst. Thus kny yearnings towards amateur smuggling were- frustrated., 1 bought a few packets of their detestable thirty centissima cig- arettes in that low little shop under the arch just out of the piazza, And as I left the place and dived into the tunnel of the Castiglione road, a ragged brigand foilbwed me, touched me on the arm and exhibited a -packet a my favourite brand when we were deep in the gloom of the archway. The well remembered colour glad- dened may heart. I was willing to pay any price for it, and ask no' questions. He seemed to have but one sample two ounce packet, but quickly gueesing that he had happened on in unlimited market, he gave a low whistle which conjured out of tha darkness a 5%% artily beauty,' of a girl, who gazed lowering at ins; and produced pack- et after %packet from her strange costume, which Wits the colour of the ,Capri cliffs. These people are evidently not 'natives of this island. The sul- len sylph seemed ; deeply suspicious of me throughout the transaction, and appeared to realise its danger rridre thoroughly than the Man, who was too eager for the money to trouble his head about the risk we were all taking. You will think I am reckless to set down evidence of my own complicity on three post cards, but they are unsigned, and the story is in sections. Be- sides no one here can read English. Post cards have been invented so long now that I imagine those through whose bands they pass have given up reading -them. I sing the praise of the post card. While on tour all my correspondence is accomplished by means, of it • thus I follow the example of a celebrated statesman of my own land. There is no searching for an envelope, no sealing or sticking on of a badly gummed stamp. The post card is a notable invention, cheap and always ready to act the messenger boy. I doff my hat and salute it.

Why am f here, you are perhaps wondering. I am come hoping I may run to earth the elusive legend of the Sirens. The Fern • Grotto on the southern side of the island is reputed to have been their home, and a most excellen't marine outlook it has, situated like a lofty cathedral window in the al!, high above the waves. The concave back of the Grotto is most admirably constructed for sending the Sirenio voice far across the waters, in fact the place is ideal for the very purpose which legend as signs to it. Curiously enough men's bones have been found here, ingeniously fractured, and I think I shall do some farther excavating along the flocr of this cavern. Who knows but I may come on a mummified Siren? What a find that would be!

" Since writing to you last I have bad an amating experience, one that would have fright- ened these superstitious islanders out of their wits, had they known of it. Instead of a mum- mified Siren I seem like to discover one' as ' a going concern.' I had been taking some measure- menta in the Fern Grotto, when I heard faintly the sound of very sweet singing that seemed to come from above rather than from the inhabited plain below me. Of course sound is somewhat difficult to trace to its source, if the position of that source is unknown. This song seemed to come to me round the cliffs, that are all but un- ! scalable. I worked my way round the face of the rock in a position of some danger, and with every yard I made, the subtle tune became more distinct, appearing to float above and beyond me. The southern sea was empty except for one boat, that was coming rapidly in, flung forward by four oars, for there was no wind. This coming craft heightened the illusion as if these sturdy mariners—fishermen no doubt—were being lured inward by the Siren song. I raised a shout, and the melody instantly ceased. The siren had heard me although the boatmen could not. After a moment's pause the siren began again; but this time in a high renetrating key ; still sweet; ! a trilling lay that would carry far in this breath- less air. Indeed the distant boatmen now ap- peared to bear it, for their blades bung motion- less in the water. At some peril to my precari- ous footing, pressing close against the cliff, I t unslung my binocular and exammed the trait. It was just as I suspected; the four rowers stood • stock still. A fifth man, with his foot on the prow, bent forward with a band to his ear. The highly pitched tones continued, but they had not the 'ancient 'effect of enticing the mariners ashore. The man at the prow turned, appeared to give an order to Jul men, who drew in their oars and began to fish.- And now the singing had ceased, and could not be revived by shouting of mine, or any imitations I attempted to give of the weird melody. So I scrambled buck as well as I could. ' The siren evidently disdains competition, and ' well she may, for I never heard anything so Zn- trancing and uncanny as her mid air song. I shall make further research to-morrow, but it will be from the top of the cllff Lthink. "Another day of disappointment, and yet not siren.altogether After s , for that, hgaot vt et/. sheen d o aimed to favour me. I did not go to the tam of could a ldhilntof not b from tho the 'cliff as I purposed yesterday, but resolved to examine the rocks with my glass from the sea, so port—the Piecalo • went instead to the little Marina they call it7and engaged a boat. There was some delay abOtit the negotiations, for the I boatman insisted on accompanying tne, which I ! , would not perntitintr any gossiping native the secret nsecortetwishf the lete o for- eigner should wish to undergo either the exertion These Capri people cannot understand why a j of vowing or w &king,: However, at last I got the boat. I could hear the song floating faintly in the air, and there was another boat, a smaller craft than the one of yesterday, making in to- wards the rocks where there is no landing. This gave further versimilitude to the song of the siren, and I resolved to intercept the lone boat- man for the purpose of making inquiries. He was too expert for me. I find these Capri boats mid ears very clumsy when compared with our ski& on the Cam. The lone boatman seemed to go into the Red Grotto, and I laboriously followed him, but lie was newhere to be seen. He could easily hair° ended me if he had wanted to do so, but why should he, want to 2' He could have gone through the Turk's Grotto, which has two en- trances, and which gets its name from the fact that some Capri fishermen gave the Turks the slip by doing the very thing. this man seems to have done. As before, the siren's song changed from its plaintive tone to the shriller pitch before it ended, and for all I could teli, she get her man this time. The notes floated from some spot mid way up the cliffs, although, viluit is more probable, the 'song may have been sung by some one lying in the grass at the dizzy summit. From the boat I examined the cliff with ray glass most minutely,' hilt .-oola di.eern no resti,u. /.1-ee even for an eagle aumig sheer wall. Unfortunately she stopped singing at the very moment when I wanted her to go on, hqping the sound would be some sort of guide to. my scrutiny of the cliff. Perhaps she was , crunching her victim. Anyhow she differs from her sisters of old in remaining invisible. I shall take to the boat again to-mor- row, if the weather is fair, go further out, and come slowly in, hoping for a song all to myself. " Yesterday's resolution was annulled by a fierce Scirocco, which made boating on the south- ern side impossible. I therefore went to the top of the cliffs, but without result. There was a grand view over a whitened sea, but no boats were -visible, so my siren proves herself true sister to the ancients by wasting no music on an empty sea. I must get a long stout rope and investi- gate these cliffs at close quarters. It will be quite an Alpine experience, for the precipice certainly looks terrible enough from this altitude. "The Scirocco is still blowing, so I acquired a ' rope, a stout iron stake and a hammer. I let my- self down over the cliff in several places, but without result. It is rather exhausting work, and needs nerve as well as strength, both of which, I am thankful to say, I possess. I drive in the iron stake very securely, tie the rope round it, also very securelV, and so let myself down, Jed ..mainst the rook face bands clutehiPco

cno rope. 1 emn be abfb to earn my Jinn 'Aeon acrobat when this task is over. If I capture the siren' I may make such affluent terms with Bar- num for the two of us, that I can retire on a for- tune. an amazing chance I actually believe I have discovered the abiding place of the tnodern siren. The quest has been so interesting tnat ii has taken entire possession of me. Last night I went out in any small boat to scan the rocks in the darkness. Once I thought I heard the siren'', call, but could not be sure. Later, however, I think I discovered her in the unromantic act of striking matches. This sounds extremely prosaic, hut when you take into consideration the intense darkness of the night, her perilous perch on the face of a beetling cliff, and the strangeness of the whole affair yon will find it is not without its touch of mystery. The matehes flared for a moment, and went out, or perhaps she had a lan- tern with which Phe was signalling. She is much further west than I had supposed from her song, but how she reaches her eyrie, and bow she main- tains herself there is beyond my comprehension. There nmst be a shallow cave up aloft which gives some sort of footing, but the ledge is so lofty thiiit it is invisible front the sea. . _ _ _

" Since writing of the night light, I have bud an calm:lure which even now makes me shudder to think of. I hope it will not be repeated. I got down over the cliff with my rope, at about the spot where I supposed the light to have shown. All at once I noticed a quiver in the rope as if some unseen iperson above rile were trying to saw it asunder. I got my feet on a shelf not more than six inches wide, and just in time, for the severed rope came down in coils around me. I found myself entirely cut off from the cliff top, plastered against the rock. hardly daring to breathe. I shouted for help, hoping some boat- man below might bear me, but at last realised that if I was to be saved it must be through my own.exerlions. My Swiss rock climbing experi- ences now stood my friend. INC() against, the wall, with outspread arms I worked my way cautiously along the precipice, hoping the shelf would last my turn, until I reached the green depression which I was making for, a shallow valley in the cliff fop. Once, through exhaustion, I feared my stamina was not going to stand the strain, for I had a sort of momentary vision, which showed me my mind was not so entirely under my control as it should have been in such a crisis. I thought I saw a human face peer at me from an impossible point in the cliff above me. It was distorted with rage and hate, or so I regarded it at the time, for it was gone in a moment—a girl's face—the mirage of the siren that has been haunting my brain, and yet it was a face I had seen somewhere before, but I can- not remember where. For an instant I persuaded myself that she, and not a protruding rock, bad cut the rope. I pulled myself together and de- termined to see no more such disquieting illusions until I was safe on more level ground. I reached the valley thoroughly done up, and lay there for a long time, panting and unable to believe that the danger was past. " I am as resolute as ever to solve this puzzle. Doubtless the answer will be commonplace enough, once I haive reached it. But I shall go no more alone in this affair. I shall engage a stalwart islander or two, who will look after the rope above while I am descending, and be ready to throw me another line, should the one that supports me show signs of giving way. You shall hear news of me to-morrow, for I feel cer- tain I am going to unravel the great mystery."

These were the last words written by Professor Wallis Dent. I paid a visit to Capri, and talked with the two men who had been with Professor Dent on the day of the disaster. They were rather stupid persons wio plainly took it for granted that the rope had parted because of abrasion on a sharp rock. On this occasion the Professor had tied the rope round him, and the two men had lowered him slowly. He evidently knew something was going wrong, although they bad no suspicion of it, for he signalled them to pull Lim up again. They were about to obey, when they suddenly found the line loose in their hands, which caused them to fall backwards to the ground. I am convinced that Professor Dent had unluckily become involved in the operations of tobacco smugglers, who were running their il- licit cargoes into a cave at the foot of the cliff, bringing their tobacco probably from Malta, and distributing it from Capri to Naples and the mainland. The girl was doubtless perched in POMO slight cavern, with an extended outlook to signal by voice or light that the coast was clear. Dent's actions must have convinced the smug- glers and their watchwoman that he sought, and perhaps had obtained evidence against them. I see no reason to disbelieve that she cut the rope on each occasion, and I suspect that she shared the fate she ?tad so ruthlessly dealt out to the doomed man, for the fishers say that one night an appalling and prolonged scream was heard down the face of the cliff, followed by a crash, and silence. These devout fishers crossed them- selves, and thought this the ghostly re-enactment of the former tragedy. However, the men who were with him say that Professor Dent did not cry out as he fell, but that statement does not shake the belief of the fishers in their ghost story.

CHILD KILLED BY A MOTOR WAGGON.

Donald White, a boy of three. was knocked down and killed by a motor waggon belonging to Messrs. Crow and Taylor, of Sutton Oak, as it was going along West Derby-road, Liverpool. The waggon, it is said, was going slowly, and the child ran in front of it in pursuit of a companion.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
GLEANINGS.

A GERMAN scientific magazine describes a new registering rain gauge, in which each drop falls on a balanced arm, which dips under it, closes an electric circuit, and registers the fall. The number of drops in half a minute indicates the intensity of the shower, and curves of it can be drawn from them.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
MAN'S MOST PRODUCTIVE PERIOD.

Professor Dexter, of the University of Illinois, has made a biographical investigation, and finds that although the gecade between thirty and forty proved the most productive in the lives of his celebrities, the following decade was nearly as productive, and the next about half as productive, so that on the whole most of the world's work is done, not before, but after forty years of age.

COCAINE. Cocaine is made from the coca bush of Peru. The leaves are dried in the sun, and brayed in a concentrated solution of carbonate of soda. The powder, obtained by drying the solution, is treated with benzine or petroleum, and a solution of hydrochloric acid. The cocaine is precipitated by carbonate of soda. A kilogramme of leaves gives t from two to four grammes of cocaine.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
ACETYLENE GAS BUOYS.

The Canadian Government has adopted acetylene gas buoys in preference to those having compressed gas, which are liable to explode. The new buoys carry a charge of carbide which, when the buoy sinks to a certain depth, comes into contact with the water, and generates acetylene gas for lighting the buoy. A certain pressure of gas cuts off the water, but when more gas is wanted the Water is automatically admitted again to the carbide so as to generate it. The pressure of gas is not over throa pounds on the square inch.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ANTIDOTE TO NICOTINE.

Strychnine has been regarded as the antidote to nicotine, but M. Zalacka,n finds it ineffectual. Eserine is not much better. Nicotine possesses a stimulating and also a paralysing property, and eserine may sometimes enhance the paralysing effect. The nasturtium officinalis, on the other hand, supplies a good antidote ta nicotine. It is rich in nitrogen and iodide of iron, especially when it grows near springs containing iron and iodine. The juice of the plant obtained by crushing it and filtering, when injected into the veins in good time, neutralises the poison of nicotine.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
A NEW MAGNETIC ALLOY.

A new magnetic alloy is announced by Herr Gumlich. It contains more than 60 per cent. of copper, 20 per cent, of mangane,e, 10 per cont, of aluminium; and a trace of lead. It is said to be easily worked, and to have the coercive force of prime cast steel. its maximum permeability equals that of cast iron, and increases considerably with age. Unfortunately it will not stand heating, and a temperature of 165 deg. Centigrade permanently strips it of its magnetic propertkis. For the present, at any rate, these new magnetic metals do not seem likely to displace industrially the compounds of iron.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
A PAINT SUITABLE FOR GALVANISED IRON.

No paint at present known can be satisfactorily applied to the surface of freshly galvanished iron in the condition in which it leaves the galvanising tank, says Science Siftings. Before any paint will adhere permanently to the galvanised sheet its surface must be weathered by exposure, so as in a great measure to sacrifice the protection afforded in the galvanising process. A ready market would be found for a paint that,when applied to a galvanised sheet upon its withdrawal from the tank, would adhere firmly to and effectually protect the metallic surface.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
EXCELLENT SOLDER FOR GLASS.

Glass is one of the most universally employed bodies. It is transparent, writes a Science Siftings correspondent, and is not attacked by commonly. occurring substances. Glass only possesses one annoying drawback apart from its fragility. It is difficult to solder toge,her with other materials, wood or metal. But even in this regard necessity, It is claimed by a German authority, has finally led to the discovery of the right thing, and he affords us the following: Melt tin, and add to the melted mass enough copper, with constant stirring, until the melted metal consists of 95 per cent, of tin and 5 per cent. of copper. In order to render the mixture more or less hard, add to 1 per cent. of zinc or lead, whereby an excellent solder for glass is obtained.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
GLASS BRICKS.

The manufacture of glass bricks, controlled by the patents held by the Societe Anonyme La Pierre de Verre Garchey, appears likely to undergo an mportant development, due to some improvements made in the method of producing this material. The method of production hitherto employed has involved two heatings of the material—that is to say, the rough glass, after being subjected to a preliminary heating, is allowed to cool slightly and is then granidated by immersion in cold water. The granules are then packed into refractory moulds and again heated until a degree ot platticity is obtained,and the rough brick from the mould placed under hydraulic pressure to secure the desired shape. The process of granulating, which involves a separate heating, and therefore a double consumption of coal, has rendered the cost of manufacture high; but it is announced, on the authority of the American Consul at Havre, that M. Garchey has discovered a process for making the bricks from glass in one heating, thus saving one-half the coal expenditure. The bricks have the chemical and physical properties of glass, and it is anticipated that a greatly enlarged demand will follow on the new process of manufacture.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MYSTERY OF N-RAYS.

Though the nature and origin of N-rays may still be said to be wrapped in mystery,. says a writer in the Electriml Magazine, yet the phenomena which reveal their presence are of the simplest possible kind, requiring no special scientific apparatus for their production. If the N-rays have any real objective existence, then anyone has it in his power to conjure them forth from their hiding- places and bid them reveal their secrets; and as the searcher has a vast field before him, who knows but that he may be instrumental in bringing to light some new and valuable discovery? Even should he not succeed in this, still the study of such mysterious radiationa will amply repay the effort requirel, which calla for all the mental and moral faculties of the observer, and constitutes an invaluable scientific training. The universal in- terest which has centred on N-rays ever since the date of their presumed discovery may be traced to several reasons, some of which have perhaps no direct connection with pure natural philosophy. Many critical inquirers in this domain appear to be hoping for the advent of some new discovery which Nvill upset one of the dogmas of science, or afford some confirmation of their own dreamy theories. Even renowned physicists have of late been drift- ing, unconsciously perhaps, into metaphysics.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
MAGNETO-CATHODIC RAYS.

A discovery of great importance- seems to have been made by M. Villard, says the Atheneum. By interposing a diaphragm with a small central open- ing in front of the cathode in a Crookes tube, and then placing the tube between the poles of a powerful electro-magnet,he produces a new kind of rays which he Galls the magneto-cathodic. These are entirely magnetic in their nature, and prove, when sep4rated by a Faraday's cage, not to be carriers of electricity, while they increase in light and length with the intensity of the magnetic field. They are deviated by an electric field of sufficient intensitF, and tend to place themselves perpendicu- larly to it. Or the pencil of ordinary cathodic rays can be made by magnetic deviation to take the form of a helix, or corkscrew, when the magnetic rays will then figure as straight lines parallel to the axis of the helix. Moreover, M. Villard has been able to sliew that, given a magnetic field of sufficient intewsity, the ordinary cathodic rays may be made to describe a perfect circle, thus return- ingto the cathode, while the magnetic-cathodic raytiLpersist in their course through the leegth of the tube. It is, therefore, claimed that these last cannot arise from any transformation of electric energy, but have an actual independent existence of their Own; and it is even argued that they must consist of streams id particles called, by analogy with those makit.g up the cathode streams, magnetons. This may be premature, adds the Arlieneram, but we may certainly admit M. Villaiars eontention that these experiments for the first time demonstrate visibly an action of the electric field at right angles to its direction and his experiments cannot fail to cast much light on the relations Zhattrsees electricity and magnetism.

CANAL TRAGEDY.

Fatal Fascination of " Jacksharps."

The inquest on the two boys, John and Walter M ata aged ten and five years, a 80, Corporation-street, who were drowned on Saturday in the canal at Blackbrook, was held on Wednesday by Mr. Brighouse at St. Helens Town Hall. Mrs. Myatt, the mother, said the elder boy was very fond of fishing, and on Friday she chastised him for going fishing in the canal. William Leivesly, collier. of :34, Blackbrook-road, Haydock' said that on Saturday afternoon he heard a scream, and walked along the canal side for about 200 yards. He then saw a boy disappearing, and himself jumped in to try to get him out. He failed, and, becoming exhausted, had to be. assisted ont.—Richard Bourne of 5, Cam-lane, spoke to- ; dragging the boys out sometime later.—The Coroner ' said it seemed clear that the elder boy had taken his brother with him, and they seemed to be fascinated by the pursuit of fishing, although the catch only meant a few jacksharps." If they only knew, probably the factsw i were that the younger boy fell n, and that the older boy made a courageous attempt to save him, and so lost his life as welL—The jury returned a verdict of 'Accidentally drowned."

Cpcling Gossip.

BY THE CLUBMAN:

Run to is perhaps the most satisfactory finish of a week- end cycling excursion, and gives a sense of " some- thing atteMpted, something done," which is not gained to anything like the same extent when the ride finishes at an inland town or village. ===== 0002601_19050617_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
Universal Lights.

All cyclists who ride by night beyond the limits of illuminated towns will feel the deepest regret at the defeat of the Universal Lights Bill, 1905, by a majority of one, on its second reading in the Com- mons on 12th May. The Cyclists' Touring Club has laboured for years to procure legislation of a uniform character, making it incumbent on all wheeled vehicles to carry lights between one hour after sunset and one hour before sunrise. Yet no less a person than Mr. Labouchere, who with several other Members opposed the Bill, declared that the measure was devised mainly in the interests of motorists. Wheelmen who realize the dangers which it is sought to mitigate by the Bill referred to should not miss the opportunity of extracting a pledge from their parliamentary representative which the approaching General Election will afford.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
The Dust Nuisance.

It is satisfactory to observe that the County Councils in many parts of the country are bestirring themselves to mitigate the dust nuisance. Various substances are being experimented with, some Producing quite unexpected results. For example, near the Hadley Stone, north of Barnet, a stretch of highway has been recently coated with a preparation of tar. Cyclists passing over the road became aware that their tyres were picking up a Spray of tiny black particles, which, in default of mudguards, were deposited on their backs. Unlike road grit, which can be shaken or brushed off, these tenacious particles left a black smear when touched, and practically ruined any light garment on which they fell. Anything more' exasperating to a cyclist oould hardly be devised than an antidote of this nature, far worse in its effects than the evil sought to be removed.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
The Ripley Road.

The rapid growth in the number of motorists using the Portsmouth road, noted for its fine scenery and excellent service, has wrought a strik- ing change in its popularity amongst cyclists. Formerly the highway which led through Ripley and Guildford to the wild beauties of Hind Head teemed with wheel-folk every week-end. The Atgel at Ditton and the Anchor at Ripley ,saw aftemblages of all the notabilities in cycling circles, while riders of every class from far and near steered the course down the famous road. Many a fierce dust-up took place over the ten miles known as " between the houses," and abundant accommoda- tion awaited the wayfarer at the favourite inns. Now all is altered. Although sparse gatherings of the small fry of wheeldom may be seen sometimes at Ditton, the high-powered cars which sweep along the road have virtually driven away the humbler pilgrims" to find a refuge from the dusty and deteriorated surface in the sequestered by- ways.

In Leafy June.

• With the leafy month of lengthy days are i- associated several annual events which excite much ' interest in cycling circles. Thus, on the 10th inst. , the votaries of distance riding have their yearly : chance of taking part in an organized ride from London to York. Starting from the G.P.O. at mid- night, and observing a steady schedule speed of 12 to 14 miles an hour, the party will reach "York at , 8.30 P.M. Then on the 24th inst. two prominent Axtures will clash. One is the 'Woodford Meet, ' beloved of East-End cyclists, who annually array themselves in divers costumes and parade pro- cessionally in aid of local hospital charities. This really pretty spectacle is admirably managed and affords delight to many thousands. On the same afternoon, on the steep slope of Westerham 'Hill, Prodigious feats of hill climbing against the watch Will be witnessed by crowds of spectators. The Catford C.C. deserve every credit for the sporting sPirit they display in promoting this instructive '43rapetition each year without the incentive of a gate:
