===== 0002601_19050617_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
Inferior Pump Connections.

Many complaints have been rife of late concern. lug the defective quality of pump connections sold to riders. Not only are the threads so roughly cut that air escapes, but the flexible tube is in- capable of sustaining the pressure of air without giving way. The best connections are those encased in a plaited fabric. The cheap celluloid pump of doubtful make proves but a sorry substitute for a genuine article by Lucas or Bluemel, which will render useful service for years.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
The Use of Toe-clips.

It is generally conceded, even by their users, that toe-clips are detrimental to perfect pedalling. That is to say that their use discourages ankling—drop- ping of the heel at the beginning of the stroke and raising it at the end. Still, thousands of riders use toe-clips to-day on the road to obviate having their feet thrown off the pedals when riding fast over rough ground, and if the clips are so arranged as to leave the feet in an easy position on the pedals, little harm should attend their use. Various patterns of leather clips have jumped into favour of late. These answer well for fast road work, but are less suited than steel clips to the needs of a traffic rider, whose feet should always be at liberty for a lightning dismount when necessary.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL

Corporation Contracts Question.

Mr. J. EDWARDSON presided at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council which was held in Bold-street Wesleyan schools on Tuesday evening. There was a fair attendance of delegates. CORPORATION CONTRACTS.

Councillor STOTT (secretary) referred to the action of the Town Council in regard to the fair wage clause. He pointed out that prior to the meeting of the Council a letter was sent to each member asking him to vote against the committee's "standard rate recommendations rucl to support Councillor Stott's proposal of a trade union rate in all contracts. As they knew, tbe money spent on the letters was really wasted. Not quite so many members as he thought had promised to support the trade union rate when circularised before the Municipal Elections. Some gentlemen who made promises were not in the Council at the present time. The following was a list of the members who actually promised to support the trade union rate :—Councillors Brongh, Barton, Benson, Clegg, Wilkinson. Forshaw, Skelland, Ellison, and Pemberton. It was only fair to state that Councillors Brough and Burton were unable

to, attend the meeting, Councillor Brough being in America. Councillor Stott explained that the Council Chamber began to empty so rapidly when the ordinary business had been transacted that he had no opportunity of moving his special resolutions on the 'question. The Trades Council had, however, gained something, as in future the mem- bers of the Town Council would take a great deal more interest in the contracts than they had been in the i habit of doing, and be more careful n ascertaining the conditions which obtained under the flans who received contracts. It was very unsatisfactory from the trade union point of view to have to submit to the "standard rate "clauses, but he did not think they could expect to gain anything better at present. Mr. T. SMITH said be was very pleased to see the position Councillor Stott had taken up on the question, There were a few councillors who could not see the difference between the trade union rate and standard rate, but workingmen could very quickly see the difference.

Mr. GREALEY said the Trades Council ought not to tamely submit to the present clauses. They should make preparations with a view to getting candidates to pledge themselves in support of trade union cionditions at the November elections. He moved that the matter be referred to the Executive Committee. .

Mr. JOLLEY seconded and said it wag disappointing to find that the promises made by candidates were unfulfilled. Perhaps before long there would Ma test case which would show whether the standard rate really meant the trade union rate. _

Councillor STOTT said the members of the Town Council who pledged themselves to support trade union clauses thought they had fulfilled their promises by voting for tbe standard rate. It was decided to leave the mattes in the hands of the Executive Committee. LETTER FROM MR. R. PIERPOINT, M.P. In reply to a letter from the Trades Council asking him to oppose the Bill in Parliament which proposes to take away the, powers of local medical officers in regard to accidents, Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P., wrote :—I have to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 15th ult., which will receive my attention. The question involves, is one which is far from easy and I am not at present prepared to give an opinion upon it. Probably you are aware that the Bill has not been read a second time.

LABOURERS AND CORPORATION WORK.

Mr. T:Smitii -said he had been asked whether it was true 034 labourers were being employed to do painting and repairs (tradesmen's work) in the electricity and tramways department of the Corporation. I Councillor STOTT said the Engineers' Society had complained of pertain work being done by men who were not fitters, but they were informed by the depart- ment that no such work was being performed by labourers. The Coachmakers' Society had written on two or three occasions, alleging that the trains were being repaired and painted by unskilled labour. He had made investigations on behalf of the men, but had not been able to get satisfaction. They wanted the depart- ment to employ a qualified coachmaker and pay him the standard rate of wages. Councillor Stott promised to make further inquiries into the matter.

A lively discussion was engendered by the reading of a letter from Mr. W. Bullock resigning his seat on the Executive. _ .

_ Mr. T. SMITH contended that before accepting the resignation the Council ought to consult the Wire- drawers' Society which Mr. Bullock represented Several members strongly dissented from this view. Councillor STorr was re-appointed delegate to the meetings of the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation of Trades and Labour Councils.

THE EDUCATION QUESTION,

The Chatham Trades and Labour Council wrote asking for the Council's support of a resolution de- claring that it is imperative. owing to the evergrowing and unequal burden of taxation. that the Education Acts should be amertdmend so that the whole cost in future might t.e borne by the national exchequer, thus paving the way to a uniform system of elementary and higher education available to all classes, Mr. ELLAM moved a resolution in support of the above. The state of education, he said,was deplorable, and under the present system he did not see much chance of its being improved. Co-ordination was the greatest necessity. Mr. JOLLEY seconded and the motion was agreed to.

The Bishops and the Sunday Liquor Traff.

Sir,—lt is said, and I believe trnly, that the brewing and distilling of intoxicating drinks cannot be carried on without Sunday labour, and candid publicans will admit that Sunday in one of their mot profitable days. Now these liquor manufactories and their 120,000 retail shops, together with the Sunday trippers who drive about in search of fresh air and the refreshments of the

public-house, must involve more than two millions of human beings, to say nothing of animals, in the total loss of the rest which they should enjoy on the seventh day of every week. This turning of the day which God % intended for a blessing to His creatures into a curse cannot fail to attract the attention of the Bishops, those . . .

highly salaried conservators of the State relidio'n, and it will be deeply interesting to notice their methods of dealing with it. , No longer ago than the 29th of May last His Grace of Canterbury brought into the House of Lords what he very rightly described as "a very modest measure," the ales of which was to restrict Sunday drinking to two hours. But thinking perhaps, that he had gone too far in the direction of reform, he immediately afterwards tacked a little and declared that " No one in his senses

would dream of bringing about a condition of things which would constitute a grievanoe to a large number of English citizens," Or, in other words, the man would be a fool who, in obedience to a Divine command, would shut the doors of the public-house on Sunday against its customers ! His Right Reverend brother of Chichester was equally outspoken. He boldly declared that" He would not close thepublic-houses altogether, even if he had the power to-morrow, but they ought to be conducted under 'restrictions whic seemed co be good to those for whose convenience they were opened." The interpretation of this is, that the publicans and their customers should themselves have the power , of opening and closing in their own hands. And his Grace of London followed in the same

spirit. His counsel was, "Let the working man have his Sunday beer to drink at home by all means but let them (by the bill) try to stop the 'soaking 'that went on in public houses betweenene and three on Sundays" What a humiliating picture of Sunday afternoon in the tap room is suggested by the Bishop's verb ! Comment en this Episcopal pleading for the publican and his friend the workingman is not required. The language is plain and indicates as clearly as words can do that the Bishops desire the continuance of the Sunday traffic although they are well aware, as who is not, that it openly tramples upon one of the most beneficent laws of God, and leads poor frail men into the paths of ruin. But how amazing and painful it must seem to Chris- tian men that the Bishops had not one word to say on behalf of Him whoseduly accredited ministers they pro- fess to be. Not only was His name excluded from the debate, but the very commandment by which He forbids all Sunday work and trading was absolutelyignored ! I am always averse to imputing motives as thelAlmighty alone can search the springs of all our actions. But in this most important instance the reasons for the Bishops' apparent neglect of dutyto God have an earthly origin and lie on the surface., Whatever doubts I may have regarding their claim too heavenly calling. I am certain that they are the servants of the King of Eng- land from whom they received their offices, and to whom, on bended knees, they do haulage. Now it happens that the King's Goverment is very largely in- terested in the liquor traffickers on account of the revenue which they derive from them. A wordly policy therefore, prompts the Bishops to be generous to the trade" in order that they may secure the confidence and favour of the king and his ministers. But what a policy !—Yours truly, lONA. _

June, 1905. P.S.--I am indebted to " The Manchester Guardian" of the 30th ult., for the quotations in this letter.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
Religious Instruction--- Rev. Morley Stevenson's Reply.

Sir,—The Rev. H. Dunnico has referred at some length to a sermon which I preached a short time ago on this subject. I am anxious to make my utterances on this question clear and unambiguous and will there- fore again ask for some of your valuable space for a reply which I will make as brief as possible. On the general question I think Mr. Dunuico has misunderstood my attitude. He writes as if my object was to magnify the Church of England and belittle the work of other bodies. Nothing: was further from my intention. I was addressing a Church of England congregation and was trying to shOw them that our Church had done much for the cause of religious education in the past and that we should be prepared to carry on the same work in the future. At the same time I endeavoured to remove some misapprehensions concerning the Educa- tion Act of 1902. Let rue repeat again what I stated in my sermon that I gladly recognize the valuable educa- tional work which has been done by the British and Foreign School Society, the Roman Catholics, the Wes- leyans and others. Ido not wish to compare one with another. In this matter we,as citizens of a great empire andas subjects of the King of Kings, carry on the work of education as a high and sacred duty. Whoever will help in this work is welcome, be his creed what it may. So far, generally. Now let me allude to some of the special points Mr. Dunnico has raised. Mr. Dunnico does not agree with me that the S.P.C.K. in the 17th century did a great work for the education of the people and that the work became so great that early in the next century the National Society was formed to carry it on. Ido not know why be doubts this. Ile will find it described in Overton's Life in the English Church 1661-1714" and perhaps I may add the following short quotation from Lecky's "England in the Eighteenth Century" Vol. 11. p. 516. One of the most important functions of the Society for the Promotion of ChristiPn Knowledge was the establishment of Charity Schools which multiplied rapidly under Anne ln the fifteen years ending in 1712 as many as 117 schools were set up in London and Westminster and nearly 5,000 children were taught in them." .

Let: it be remembe;ed that these schools, which in 1712 numbered 500 throughout the country, educated, clothed and fed the children without tiny payment, grant or rate. The whole cost was borne by voluntary contributions. That the National Society grew out of the S.P.C.Ii. is clearly stated in "Elementary Education " published by the National Society and unless Mr. Dunnico has any proof to offer to the contrary, I must maintain the accuracy of what I said. I am obliged to him, however' ' for pointing out the slip I made in stating that the Society was founded in 1801 instead of 1811. I do not know how I came to make the mistake, though for the purpose of my argument, it is not material. Mr. Dunnico's account of the foundation of the British and Foreign and the National Societies seems to me one-sided and I cannot agree with his view. But this is a matter of opinion and I am only concerned with facts.

/cannot notice all the points raised bylAr,Dannico con- cerning the money collected for the building of schools. am not coacerned just now with the Sunday School question. lam glad to hear that Free Churchmen have given so generously and consider that they are entitled to great credit for what they have done. I am quite aware that some of the funds contributed by Com- panies have been given .with the primary object of staving off a school-board rate, but having made all allowance for these and other facts I still abide by my opinion that £50,000 does not exaggerate the amount given by Church people in this town for the building of schools. 1 say again it is a gift for which we, of the present day, should be grateful and one which should be an example to us and to those who come after us. Lastly Mr. Dunnico thinks lam wrong in saying that the teaching of the great days of the Christian year could not be given in an Huidenominational school. I am delighted to hear it and accept his correction with pleasure. Every point on wEic,h we can establish agree- ment brings us nearer to unity. It does not, however, touch my contention that tindenominational teaching leaves many things untaught which we feel strongly ought to be taught. lam sorry that Mr. Dunnico has referred to Mr. Boucher's unfortunate book. lam sure that he is too fair and generous a controversialist to have done so, had he known the circumstances. Mr. Boucher was for A011:10 years Principal of a Training College. He was asked to send in his resignation because the committee disapproved of the teaching he gave on the relations of the Church to Nonconformity. Some years after his resignation he published his book. It has never been used in any college, and has been condemned on the highest authority. It cannot be said to be a fair repre- sentation of the teaching given by the church of England, and ought not to he quoted as being so. The Principal of any Training College would repudiate the passages quoted by Mr. Dunnico, as strongly as he would himself. I cannot understand the last paragraph of Mr. Dunnico's address:—"The schools belonged to thepeople were paid for by the people "&e. What does this mean?' confess Ido net know. Every child and every teacher . . freo fromall sectarian tests." What sectarian test is imposed upon any child? I do not know. Ido not care for any political victory, but I do care for the religious education of the children. I believe it will be anevil day when under the colour of banishing sectarian tests, all religious teaching is driven out of our schools, and I trust that day may never come.—l am, sir, your obedient servant,

MOI3UY STEVENSON.

DRUIDS CONFERENCE. Motion for the Exclusion of Warrington Delegates BRO.)NWID HOUGHTON ELECTED GRAND MASTER.

'The annual conference in connection with the order of Druids was opened at Crewe on Tuesday. Prior to the conduct of the ordinary business there was some con- siderable discussion as to the position of the Warring- ton ' delegates, the district accounts not having been paid. The rule relating to the subject ordered that no district whose accounts were not paid should be allowed •to send delegates to the conference unless a satisfactory reason was given for the non-payment of the account. The Grand Secretary read a letter sent by the War- rington District to the Board of Management, in which the reasons for the attitude assumed by the district were set forth. They complained that fair treatment had not been meted out to them by the Board, and that the latter had on that account lost the confidence of the district. . . . .

Bro. Smith (Newcastle) moved "That in the opinion of the delegation the explanation gives by the Warring- ton district be not considered satisfactory, and that consequently its delegates be not allowed to sit." The Warrington District said that the Board had lost its confidence, and yet he found it was making an appeal to the,Order that had forfeited its confidence. If the Dis- trict intended to make au appeal, or have any dealings with them, it ought first of all to have kept itself within the letter of the law—(hear, hear)—and made its dele- gates eligible. As it had failed to do so be begged to move that the rule be carried out, and that the Warring- ton delegates he not allowed to sit. A Glasgow delegate seconded. The Grand Treasurer said he should perhaps surprise them by asking them not to pass the resolution. He would give them his reasons. They had upon their busi- ness sheet an important matter, of which it had been truly said, they had seen the first that morning. The mere fact that such an appeal was before their gathering should lead them to give the Warrington district every opportunity—for full and clear investigation into the subject. The Board were on their defence, and as a member of the Board he could say that they were per- fectly clear and agreed on the subject. The Board knew that they had a clean sheet before them. The mere exclusion of the delegates from Warrington could simply be regarded as a somewhat harsh act, and he himself would far rather strain a point in the other direction than be accused of unfairness. (Hear, hear.) Let them be fair to everybody; and though he was perfectly aware, and agreed with Brother Mellor and everybody else that the rule had been broken—and he would punish the breach in a way—let them say this that whatever money was owing the Board could find means of compelling its payment. He appealed on behalf of the weak in this 'case, and asked them to allow the Warrington delegates to sit. Bro. Mellor said that with all clue respect to the Grand Treasurer there was a principle at stake and the rules should be strictly adhered to. (Hear, hear.) The fact of carrying out the rule would not exclude the evidence which the appeal was to bring before them. He would be the last one to exclude anyone from a hearing, but he contended if they were to allow breaches ' of rules in this way they did not know where they would land themselves? (Hear, hear.) The motion ef Bro. Mellor was put to the vote, and was declared lost.

THE ELECTION OF OFFICERS. The proceedings of this delegation were continued on Wednesday, in the Co-operative Hall, Crewe, Mr. R. fl. Mason, Grand Master, presiding. The report of the Grand Secretary was received and adopted. It stated that during 1904 twenty-two new lodges were opened in England, Wales, and Scotland, and all were doing well. The remainder of the morning sitting was taken up by a discussion on a notice of appeal lodged by the Warrington district against a decision of the Board of Management reinstating If prominent member of the Order who had been expelled the district for alleged offences while acting as a district officer. It was stated that the parties immediately con- cerned bad met and agreed to refer all maiters in difference, including a law suit, to arbitration, but this did not satisfy several members, who held that all charges personal to the officer in question should be withdrawn, and ultimately the matter was postponed for a further conference. The greater part of the after- noon was taken up in hearing the appeal, which resulted in the action of the board being confirmed by 40,015 votes against 1,495. The delegates then proceeded to the election of grand officers. Mr. David Houghton (War- rington) was elected Grand Master. There were four candidates tor the Vice-Grand Master- ship, and the choice fell upon Mr. G. H. Hall (Sheffield). Mr. John Wagtail, the retiring Grand Secretary, was re-elected, and the vacant Grand Treasurership was filled by the appointment of Mr. R. V. Clayton, the acting treasurer. The elections of members of the board of management and junior auditor stood over till to-day. In the evening the delegates were entertained at dinner by the Crewe District, Dr. Wilson presiding.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WHITSUNTIDE HOLIDAYS.

With such ideal weather conditions as favoured the Whitsuntide holidays it was little wonder that many Warringtonians went to seek enjoyment at seaside and country resorts. Throughout the week end there was glorious sunshine, while a breeze slightly tempered the heat. Whit Sunday was as usual observed as a great festival at the Anglican and Roman Catholic Churches, some of which were very prettily decorated. On Mon- day the town was almost deserted, for there was quite a dearth of holiday attractions. The special facilities provided by the .railway companies were largely taken advantage of and the bookings were heavier than usual. From Bank Quay station 200 booked too Wellington and Shrewsbury, 450 to Chester. 500 to North Wales, 600 to Frodsham and Helsby, 500 to Blackpool, 100 to Morecambe and Windermere, and 100 to Hooton Park. The Cheshire Lines officials booked 1,800 passengers to Liverpool, 900 to New Brighton, 900 to South. port, 150 to Douglas, and 10.00 to Manchester Cyclists were able to enjoy themselves to their hearts content, and the many picturesque spots in Cheshire were visited by the majority of wheelers. Others, how- ever, ventured further afteld and spent the week-end in touring. Altogether the Whitsuntide of 1905 will be remembered as a conspicuous success.

£7B 16 1 PROCESSIONS AT RUNCORN.

At Runcorn on Whit Monday the usual Friendly ' Society and Sunday school demonstrations took place. The procession of Friendly Societies in the morning WM witnessed by large crowds, and the tableaux and other pleasing features of the various lodge displays came in for much admiration. Equally successful were the three processions of Church of England, Noncon- formist and St. Edmunds' Roman Catholic Sunday schools. The Parish Church School assembled at the National Schools in Church-street, and was there joined by Holy Trinity Church School, Mill Brow Mission School, St. Michael's School. and the Mersey Mission to Seamen's School. Proceeding by the route of Regent-street, High-street, Bridge-street, Heath-road, Victoria-road, and Greenway-road, they visited the Vicarage where a brilliant scene was enacted on the lawn, the Volunteer Band and the band of the training ship Indefatigable playing appropriate selections. Afterwards the chil- dren dispersed to an adjoining field where games were indulged in. Eventnally they were marched back to their respective schools and provided with tea. The Nonconformist schools also made a pleasing and lengthy procession. They paraded the town in the following order :—Baunswick, St. John's Bethesda, Greenway- road Primitive Methodist, St. Luke's, Halton-road Wesleyan, United Methodist Free Church, Camden, Nelson-street ‘Vesleyan. Ashbridge-street Primitive Methodist, and the Salvation Army. Brunswick were headed by the Volunteer Band, while the Salvation Army(who conducted a special mission all Whitsuntide) had the assistance of a number of instrumentalists from Liverpool. St. Edward's School, made a very pretty picture, the dressing of the younger children being admirable. They were headed by their own band.

Temperance Work at Southport Shore On Wednesday the Police Court Missionaries of the C.E.T.S. attended the above with their tert and made and distributed free nearly one hundred gallons of the temperance drinks, hopkos and stokos. The drinks were made on the spot by Messrs. Taylor and Gold- stone. Very many expressed themselves favourably with regard to the drinks " very good," and "first class " being the general expressions of opinion. Several thousand recepies were also given away. These drinks are now being very much used by farmers and in large works in place of alcholic drinks,

Warrington visited Preston on Thursday and' the friendly match resulted in a draw. A capital wicket had been prepared, such as the average batsman delights to operate on, and winning the toss Mr. E. A. Connor decided to take advantage of it. The chief feature of the Warrington display was the brilliant hitting of the captain who in the course of an hour pat together 77. He hit one six and nine fours. On the Preston side Chew was the best batsman, coin- piltng 59 in forty minutes. He also came out with a capital bowling average, taking 3 wickets for 14 runs.

Bowling. Local greens received their full share of patronage last week-end despite the general exodus from the town some good Vplay was witnessed. There was a cessation of hostilities in connection with the League clubs on account of the holding, of the second of a series of individual mains. This was opened on the Bull's Head green on Saturday and concluded on Tuesday evening, R. Rothwell claiming the honours of the day by defeating J. Bolton.

The fixtures in connection with the Warrington and District League will be resumed to-day. The stiffest struggle will rekit between Sankey and Fearnhead who meet on the Chapel House Green. In the other two matches the home teams, Bull's Head and Horse and Jockey will almost certainly defeat respectively the Penketh and cemetery representatives.

Another interesting fixture was decided on the Swan Hotel Green on Saturday. when 32 married and single men of the neighbourhood had an exciting struggle, the victory resting with the married men by 10 points. The bachelors started well and as the result of the first two games had a lead of 13 points. Five victories to the married men placed them in arrears but they were not pointed to remain for long in a disadvan• tageous position, as four games later they held a substantial lead. The decisions in the next three encounters reduced their lead to one of two pints and naturally all was excitement when the last two men entered the green. W. Holloway successfully upheld the honour of the married men and defeating F. Dooley 11-0 gave his side the victory.

In connection with the Works Competition further progress was made in the contest for the Finlayson cup. Rylands Bros. (No. 1) and Crosfield's met in the first round, the former team qualifying for the second stage in the competition. A lead gained by Crosfield's as the result of the first game was almost immediately lost and for the remainder of the match they were unable to wrest the advantage for their opponents who ran out winners by 27 points. Rylands team have however, probably run their race as their chances of beating the Pearson and Knowles team in the second round are far from rosy.

Cycling.

The Whitsuntide tours proved highly successfuLfor the weather was ideal and the roads were in excellent condition. About twenty members of the Warrington Cycling Club visited Ellesmere and the Vale of Dan- gollen and the outing must rank among tha most successful ever organised by the club. Me outward journey was made via Chester, Erbiscock, Overton and Bangor-on-Dee and Saturday night was spent at the Red Lion at Ellesmere. On Sunday the party went on to Oswestry, where a halt was made for lunch, and afterwards rode through GobYwen and Chirk to Llan- gollen, putting up at the Evion Hotel. On Monday the machines were mounted early, and after a short stay in Chester, the cyclists varied the route to Warrington by calling at °Luton Park, where several hours were pleasantly spent at the military fete. For a continuous round of pleasure the tour has perhaps never been excelled in the history of the club.

On Monday a good muster of club members who had not joined the touring party visited Oulton Park. Delamere Forest is at present cooking at its best, and the ride through the beautiful • lanes, almost arched with trees, was thoroughly appreciated. Owing to the unsettled weathe there was only a srattll turnout for the run to Tabley on Tuesday evening.

Sandiway was the destination of the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan C.C. on Saturday, and a dozen members enjoyed a spin to that pretty village. On Whit Mon- day a very pleasant time was spent at Chester.

GRAPPENHALL PARISH COUNCIL.

County Council Expenditure.

A meeting of the Grapenhall Parish Council was held on Tuesday evening in the schools, Grappenhall, when there were present Messrs. Lister-Kaye (in the chair) Jenkins, Waring, Rathbone, Ellison, Warburton, SAnce, Lieutenant Avery and S. J. Woodcock (clerk). ..

===== 0002601_19050617_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNTY COUNCIL EXPENDITURE.

The Clerk-wd-that, as instructed, he had written to the Clerk of the County Council and the Runcorn Rual District Council with respect to the increase of county expenditure, and had received a reply from the County Council stating that the Council were giving considera- tion to the question and that the increa.sed duties im- posed by Parliament on County Council involved addi- tional expenditure without any corresponding grants having been made in aid thereof. Mr. Jenkins said he was pleased to find that local bodies were taking the matter up. He had also been pleased to.flnd that Mr. Hallows, the representative of Stockton Heath or the District Council, spoke particu- larly strongly on the ppint. He was,however,rather sur- prised to find him going back on his word when he said that he did not see how the Runcorn Rural District Council could effect any saving. They were, he thought,'very greatly indebted to Mr. J. Charlton Parr for the step he took in bringing the resolution he did before the Ches- hire County Council. There were some remarks made by certain gentlemen there wlaich were anything but gentlemanly. (flear, hear). He moved " That this Council desires to place on record its thanks to Mr. Parr, the chairman, for having brought forward the resolution against the excessive expenditure of the County Council, and trust that the success attending the passing of that resolution by the County Council will be seen in a speedy curtailment of the expenses and a corresponding reduction in the rates." (Hear, hear). Lieutenant Avery seconded, and the motion was carried unanimously. THE QUESTION OF ASSESSMENT. The Clerk read a letter from the County Council stating that the County Rate Basis Committee proposed to revise the county rate basis now in force for Grappenhall for the purpose of meeting the partial changes that had occurred in the rateable value of property situated therein, and, subject to say objections that might be made thereto, to alter such basis or standard and to fix the same at £9,633 the assessable value under the Agricultural Rates Act, being £8,647. The Clerk stated that their gross valuation was £10,144 ss. During the past half year they had increased the valuation by £5OO. They were about £2OO less to be assessed on than the valuation was so that they were on the right side. (Ilea; bear).

[By Mr. H. Whitham.]

Two thousand miles away from home. In the midst or slaughterdom, where I can hear, of the ways and doings of men bent, not on making a mere competence, but whose every energy is devoted to a soul-enslaving life-long chase of the everlasting dollar, —not only can I hear,but being very favourably located for seeing all that the ' Picking business means I may be able to say a word in defence of the ' Packer ' as well as vent my scorn of him as a dollar chaser. For the English reader, it may be desirable to explain that a 'Packing ' house is simply a slaughter house, and the boss, however rich he may be, just a butcher and nothing else. Leaving California on the 17th May, we, my wife and I, reached Nebraska on the 20th, riding night and Iday over long dreary and wearying stretches of prairie land. Not a tree, pot a shrub to be seen. Years ago the buffalo roamed these prairies, now nothing but the coyote and the jack rabbit find an existence possible with the wild sage bush. Possibly the near future may alter all this. We have in Cali- fornia a magician of the name of Burbark. Having done wonderful things with spuds, fruits, and flowers, he has turned his attention to the prickly cactus. This seemingly useless prickly plant finds its habitat on the sandy wastes, and to a limited extent ventures on the prairie. Mr. Burbark is sure to be successful in robbing this plant of its prickles. He will make it produce large pads of moist flesh-producing meat, and the prairie of the.future having lost the buffalo, will feed the cattle future, Englishmen will eat. Irrigation will step in and a neyr world will be discovered. Riding in a tourist car for two thousand miles at a stretch is quite an experience. One makes acquaintance with all sorts and conditions of men, and it was my good fortune to have some long and interesting talks with one, who, as breeder, feeder and salesman, knew well of what he was speaking. A man of considerable culture, he could describe the conditions without introducing any bitter feeling. He had managed to look after number one, and not having run against ruin had kept his Balance, and the following story, while not my travel- ling friend's, is very similar in most of its details, and I give it with every confidence in its truth. When a man's years are verging on the allotted span of the .Psalmist he is often tempted to turn his mind's eye on the past and do some big thinks. Take all that is meant by the word competi)- tion. The times and times the word has slipped off my pen nib, and how I have abused it. Now that I see in the survival of the fitterst the death-blow being given to the word and its ways. I wonder that the resources of civilization had no easier way of arriving at a desired end. Was it not possible to kill com- petition without the attendant misery the Trusts have brought about? It certainly was. But that's another story.

M. L. E. Russell, writing of the Beef Trust's opera- tions in the cattle markets and upon the vast cattle industries of the West, shows how the destruction of competitive buying impoverished farmers, ruined cattle raisers, drove banks into liquidation and men to suicide. He tells how the Trusts inflicted losses estimated at 12,500,000 dots., in lowa and 10,000,000 dols., in South Dacota, and how the markets were manipulated and controlled for Trust profits. He relates how it came about that one buyer was all buyers and one market all markets In a word, how " success " was gained and the fittest survived. .

The success of Gustavus Swift with his refrigerator car overturned the whole world's methods of food supply. Formerly, the corn to feed cattle had been carried from Western farms to the East ' • now on the farms where it had been grown it was fed to cattle that were slaughtered not far away, and only the net result in dressed beef must needs be transported. Indiana and Colorado ceased to be dependant on the corn market and turned their attention to cattle. Com- munities that once had lived by selling corn cow became corn buyers. From this point we go a little forward and touch or notice the raising of cattle, the building up of the framework,and on to the "finishing" or feeding on corn for six or eight months when they are delivered to Chicago, Omada, Kansas City, St. Joseph, or St. Louis to be slaughtered. Here the question of capital strikes us. To have range-cattle the farmer must buy and ship them to his farm. This needs 'capital and the farmer must in most cases be carried by his local bank, until he effected the sale of his fat cattle. A. great- business grew up for the country bank. Now we get on the trail of blood, and remember that while corn gets higher in price, the cost of the workman's dinner also climbs up, yet, all the time, the selling price of cattle is going down, because the bid of one firm of buyers was the bid of all firms, One market was all markets.

From July 1903 to July. 1904 the average loss on every head of cattle fattened and sold in the Middle West was eight dollars. The farmer's broad, patient back bore the losses, the collapse of his hopes, and even the mortgage, just as it had borne many other such loads ; but involved in the catastrophe were two factors not equally adapted to load-carrying. One was the country bank, the other the cattle-shipper. One has to be familiar with the small country towns that spread themselves through the cattle raising and cattle feeding States, to know how their prosperity depends entirely on the prosperity of the farmers. Farmer and shipper to do business must borrow money at the looal bank. When at the end of 1902 the Trust fully possessed the cattle markets and so hammered it down that shipments ceased to be profit- able, the enormous losses fell upon the shipper as well as farmer. But the shippet could not go to the farmer mortgage ; he was tight against the wall. He went into bankruptcy. Whereupon the local bank found itself in a tight place. The farmers could not pay their notes, the shippers were ruined. Scores upon scores of men who from boyhood had grown up 'to be the pillars of society, of the small churches of these prosperous little towns, owing no man anything, forming strong links in the chain that held the town- ship together were smashed. The Trusts were merciless, The South Dakota Historical Society in its annual review of the progress of the State says: —" The machination of the Beef Trust almost destroyed the flourishing live stock industry of South Dakota." This is but a cold record of an historical fact.

In September, 1904, a Mr. Franklin made a large shipment to Omaha of choice stock. When his cattle were exhibited in the Omaha yards he was astonished to find that be received,but one bid for them, and that so low that its acceptance left him with a loss. Finding this condition held firmly and protest useless, he reshipped his cattle from Omaha to Chicago. When they were pit on view at the Chicago stockyards he found that he had had his labour for his pains. There was only one bid at Chicago, and that was at pre- cisely the figures offered him at Omaha. He could take what was offered him, or ship his cattle home. He took the price: pocketed his loss, returned to Dakota, sold his herds for what he could get, and retired from the business. The estimated loss in South Dakota for 1904 is ten million dollars, in lowa were twelve million, and wherever in the middle west cattle sent to market there was an average loss of eight dollars a head. Now let us. follow this miserable and criminalj business to* a few of its results. Towns struck by a bank failure, shrivel away like burned paper, and men turn old in a clay or go mad. In the failure at Holstein the Fraternity of Modern Woodmen lost 100,000. dollars. At lowa Falls and Wapello, bank officers were sentenced to penal servitude. At Coin, the head of the wrecked bank is under indictment for yielding ' to the temptations of letting cattle men have money. ! In Colfax (4. D. Wood was looked up to as the soca of integrity ; he was the wealthiest, most active, and most respected citizen. The bank was his life and soul, he had built its prosperity. It had lent much to cattle-feeders and shippers and by mortgages on cattle. When the ' rumours came at the close of 1903 that the bank had lost heavily, and Mr. Wood assured his fellow-towns- men that the bank was all right, they believed Wood against the evidence of the market and maintained their deposits. On the evening of December 18th, 1903 two prominent business men of Colfax called at Mr. Wood's house and discussed with him the situation created by the stories of trouble in the bank. Mr.' Wood told them the stories were unfounded. Alter a time he rose and said in a quiet way you excuse me for a moment, gentlemen ? " Then he walked deliberately upstairs and killed himself. When the affairs of the bank were examined it was found to be ruined. The receiver sold at auction mortgage and other securities of a face value of more thon 100,000 dollars and received for them 631 dollars. More than half the firms in the town had deposited in

the bank and lost all their deposits.—At St. Charles, the cashier and principal stockholder in the citizens' bank was Charles Wood. He had directed the loaning of large sums to farmers to be used in buying and feeding cattle. When losses instead of profits began to be the rule in the cattle business many of the farmers were unable to meet their notes. The bank had been a sound, conservative business. It became hopelessly entangled in the cattle feeders troubles, went to wreck, and on February 11th, 1904 Mr. Wood shot himself. At Coining, F.L. La Rue cashier of the State bank of Coining, found that many of the loans he had made to the farmers could not be collected. He was short in his accounts. The bank failed, La Rue killed himself on January 27th, 1904. At Sigourney the cashire of the County savings bank, of which his , father was president, shot himself, because of failure, on January Bth, 1904. I could go on like this for , columns but will conclude the list with one tragedy that goes down to the elements of things. , H. L. Spencer was cashier of the Grinnell National Bank a post he had filled competently, and with credit to himself, the bank and its cashier stood exceedingly • well in the community. Mr. Spencer's son was assist-. ' ant cashier. On the evening of July 20, 1904, father and son were at Arbor Lake, not far from Grinnell. They took a naptha launch and steered out towards the middle of the lake. No one else was the with them. There was still light enough for people on shore to see what happened. Of a sudden the son was seen to arise, take off his coat, and cast himself over board. His father immediately followed. The father gained the shore, looked back and saw his son disappearing in the water, then the father plunged back, and both were drowned. At first these events were believed to be melancholy and inexplicable accidents. The bodies of father and son were brought to a stricken home. On the day of the funeral the Bank was closed as a mark of respect and esteem. The next day the community was startled to find the bank closed. It has remained closed ever since. Examination of the bank's affairs showed the cashier was short in his accounts, and had been short all the time he had held office. Twelve years before, his father, whom he succeeded as cashier, had been found dead. It was now learned that he had left a defalcation that the son had laboured twelve years to conceal. by constantly falsifying the statements of the bank's condition, and by manufacturing forged notes and securities to deceive the bank examiners. About 10,000c1o1s. worth of these forged notes ; was found. The grandson knew of the family ' inheritances of trouble. He seemed always oppressed by a profound melancholy, shunned society, and lived ; the life of a recluse. The .shortage itself was not large, but the bank was drifting and exposure meant ' ruin. It had 332,000 dollars on deposit, its chief customers were farmers. The work of the Beef Trust made it impossible for the family to pull up, and rather than meet disgrace they went to their deaths. The reign of this Power has been ruin and death. There is not one of those wealthy men, whether it be Rockefeller with Oil and his fortune of £100,000,000, I or Carnegie with iron and his fortune of £22,000,000, and his £50,000,000 given away, or the Beef men whose fortune cannot be got at, but are a disgrace to the twentieth century. In my next letter I propose to show the benefits the Trusts claim to have conferred on the general public. H. WHITHAM, Omaha.

===== 0002601_19050617_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
NATIONAL SERVICE LEAGUE. Annual Report of the Warrington Branch.

"THE MISLEADING ARGUMENT OF OUR

STATESMEN."

This (Saturday) afternoon the second annual meeting of the Warrington branch of the National Service League will be held at,Appleton Hall. The speakers Will inelude Mr. R. A. Yerburgh, M.P., Mr. George Shea, M.A., and Professor Roget. In their annual report the committee state :—lt is very gratifying to be able to report that the member- I ship has increased from 229 as mentioned in last year's I report to 393 this year. This has been largely due to the excellent work done by the Ladies' Committee which has been formed, thanks to the energy of Mrs. Edelsten. We axe very glad to announce that Lady Lilford has kindly consented to become president of the Ladies' Committee. We regret to announce that Mr. Haslara finds himself compelled to relinquish his duties as joint hon. secretary, and we take this oppor- tunity of thanking him for his work as secretary, especially during the formation of the Branch. A list of the principal meetings held during the year is given, and the report proceeds: We wish to thank especially those ladies who have been so kindas to hold drawing-room meetings. We are con- vinced more and more that it is these small gatherings which are the most successful, and next winter it is our intention to organise numerous small meetings rather than large ones. The latter are expensive, for it is necessary to advertise ; they are also apt to he duller than the former, at which discussion can more easily take place. We wish to remind members and associates that any one wishing to really work for the Lbague can become an hon. helper, receiving as such a Toucher book, by means of which he or she can give receipts to any persons willing to join, and also a roll to be tilled up with the names of those people who may not be in a position to pay a shilling, but who will give their signa- ture in favour of the League programme. Most useful work has beenalready done bysome of the hon. helpers; one has persuaded no less than thirty people to join the League. We are glad to note that other towns such as Manchester, Liverpool and Birmingham are following the lead of Warrington, and have already formed or are about to form Branches. So, slowly but surely, will the educative influence of the League make itself felt, despite the misleading arguments, of our statesmen. We have lately been told by our Prime Minster that six battleships and a few cruisers and submarines are sufficient to render any attempt at invasion futile. If thii is really so, why do we waste money and energy on the Volunteers? Surely the logical course for aoyone holding such opinion is to abolish them. We consider. however, that this doctrine of the impossibility of invasion is a most pernicious one for this country. We I do not think it unreasonable that we should be prepared to face the possibility of a combination of two Powers against us, and in the event of such a misfortune is it I not evident that the Navy will require to have as free a hand as possible so as to concentrate land attack •in overwhelming force, and that it will be fatal for her to be hampered by having to actually guard these shores ? For this we require a sound second line of defence on land, which we have not got at the present moment, and which we never shall have until we learn the lesson that home defence is the business of the citizens themselves. For other reasons besides military ones, we regret Mr. Balfour should have lulled the people's legitimate anxieties to sleep. Many of us believe that if the mili- tary need for compulsory service be not well proven, the ad oantages to the people of the discipline and physical development which would ensue from the adop- tion of the League's proposals are so great as to make it worth oar while on these grounds alone to introduce some such training. The evidence given before the Royal Commission on physical deterioration materially rein- forces this opinion, for the general opinion of the wit- nesses called may, be summarised thus :- 1. We do not definitely know whether the physique of the nation is going forward or backward, because we have no data. . . .

2. It would seem, however, that the physique of the upper classes is improving, while that of the working classes (exclusive perhaps of the artisan class) is deteriorating, due to the fact thatalthough the slums of our towns are better in quality they extend over a far greater area, and therefore affect a greater propor- tion of the population than formerly. 3. There is a lamentable lack of the virtues of taking pains, of cleanliness and of punctuality. Now, why is it that we are so little alarmed about the state of affairs ? Very largely because, as mem- tioned above, through lack of national returns on physique we have no positive data with which to focus public attention on these matters. National Service will at once supply this data and what perhaps will weigh far more with most of us, will directly help to remedy the failings of character above referred to. This being so, let .us continue without hesitation to help forward the day when the rising generation, rich and poor alike, shall be proud to cheerfully train for their country, and when patriotism and brotherhood in- duced by common service to the Motherland, will re- vitalise and beautify our national life. Let us not lose heart because of prejudice, ignorance and opposition. No great reform has ever yet been won without per- severance in the face of all obstacles. Let us laugh at discouragement and go forward determined "to strive, to seek, to find and not to yield." The statement of accounts shows a credit balance on the year's working of £4 is. 25., but on the general account there is a deficit of .£1 10s. 7d. (J.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

In consequence of the holidays, the attendance at the meeting of this society was notquite as large as usual on Sunday last. Mr. D. Owen presided, and made reference to the Jewish Feast of Tabernacle. Mr. W. H. Hill of Manchester was the speaker and spoke upon. " Finger Prints," with many illustrations of an interesting character from science and biblical history which were listened to with great interest. Mr. A. Cotterill, sang in his usual good stile," Glory to God." Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at the organ. Mr. A. Healey and the Ilev. John Yonge also took part in the meeting. The secretary announced that Mr. Frank Ford, the converted burglar, would speak at the Open Meeting Sunday Next.

M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC aids Digestion.-- (Advt.) Mr R. A. Naylor and Unionism. About 150 members of the Criccieth Ladies' Unionist Association were entertained at Plas Hen, Llanystwmdwy to tea, &c.. on Friday, by Mr. and Mrs. Egerton Bracken. After tea a meeting was held on the lawn in front of the mansion, Mr. Egerton Bracken presiding. Sir Hugh J. Ellis Nanney, Bart., said by uniting the colonies with the mother country and maintaining the integrity of the United Kingdom, the latter would be the heart of a mighty Empire. Mr. R. A. Naylor, who was very warmly received, said never was Unionism in a more flourishing state in the constituency than it was at present. (Cheers.) Whatever they might think he could say from what he had seen and heard that they were going to win the coming election. (Cheers.) The seed of discord that was being cast abroad by the Radicale would by-and-by produce a crop of bitter ill-feeling in the country. Mr. Ryman Hall, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Egerton Bracken, referred to the candidature of Mr. Naylor, who, he said, was considered as the best possible candidate for the constituency. He was a man whe had travelled a great deal, and who had used his eyes and ears to a good purpose. He knew how we were handicapped in our trade by other countries. This would all be stopped if retaliation were adopted: Mr. J. E. Smith and Mr. E. E. Owen also spike, and the resolution was carried with acclamation. UNDER THE CLOCK for Goad BOOTS.—Adyt. Local Government Board Inquiry. Colonel W. R. Slacke, R.E., held an inquiry on behalf of the Local Government Board at the Town Hall on Friday morning into the application of the Council for sanction to borrow £4,150 for purposes of electric lighting and £4,006 for the purpose of defraying expenditure in excess of the amount authorised to be borrowed under the Warrington COrporation Act, 1899 for the provision of new Police Buildings. The Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) made the application on -behalf of the Corporation He pointed out that the electricity loan was required for extensions of plant and purchase of land. He said the land was being sold to the Electricity . Committee at cost price, the Corporation having bought it some years ago at 1-d per square yard. The land was an acre in extent. —Councillor Pemberton said the com- mittee were being charged £l,OOO for an acre of land. The Town Clerk had told the inspector that the committee were having it at cost price (17,d per yard), but that would only come to about £550 and not £l,ooo.—The TOWII Clerk : There are expenses connected with it.—Councillor Pemberton : But there is a vast difference between £550 and £l,OOO.— The Town Clerk said the Corporation would , never attempt to apply to the Board to borrow more than they had paid for the land.

Warrington Man's Alleged Perjury. At the Stalybridge l'olice Court on Friday of last week against Walter Cross, a builder and contractor, of Stalybridge and Dukinfield and , formerly of War- rington, was charged with committing perjury in a statutory declaration. Mr. R. Garelde Ives, who appeared on behalf of the Director of Public Prosecu- tions, said the declaration was made before a magis- trate in November, 1903. A. Mr. Rawlinson had negotiations with the prisoner for the purchase of a house and shop. The property was freehold, and when a man was an undischarged bankrupt he could not give a title to freehold property. A statutory declaiaticar was prepared, and in that declaration Cross stated that never in his life had he been adjudi- cated a bankrupt, either on his own petition or that of any other person. He also stated that a receiving order had never been made against him, and that he had never filed his own petition, or taken the benefit of any Act for the relief of an insolvent debtor, Upon that declaration the proposed purchaser paid a deposit of £5O. Later, discoveries were made, and 'objection Was made to proceeding with the purchase unless Cross was proved by the officials in bankruptcy at Warring- ton to be not the same person whom he (Mr. Ives) bad in his mind as having been a bankrupt there. Eventu- ally Cross, who denied that he was the same person, ga‘e them the option of breaking off the negotiations, which was done. Mr. lves added that the proceedings were brought in the interests of public morality. The Bench remanded the prisoner for a week, allow- ing bail, Cross in £lOO, and two sureties of £5O each. On Wednesday at Stalybridge Police Court Cross, was again remanded on hill. Mr. It; Garside Ives, for the Director of Public Prosecutions, said he had to prefer a farther charge against the prisoner of haying ob- tained Foods to the value of more than £2O without informing the person from whom he purchased them that he was an undischarged bankrupt. • NOT TOO FAT TO RUN. After two Hoxton labourers had accepted the offer of Mrs. Hall, of Hendon, who had been shopping, to give them 4d. to carry her basket, one of them said to the other : "She is too fat. to ran; make a snatch." So ' they dropped the basket, snatched Mrs. Hall's purse,and bolted. She ran after them, however, sufficiently fast to ensure their capture by a policeman. They were remanded.
